    LFCS EXAM DOMAINS & WEIGHTS
    ───────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  LFCS EXAM BREAKDOWN (Performance-based, 2 hours)               │
    │  SOURCE: training.linuxfoundation.org — OFFICIAL DOMAINS        │
    │                                                                  │
    │  ████████████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  25%  Operations  │
    │                                                       Deployment │
    │   • Kernel parameters (persistent & non-persistent)              │
    │   • Process & service management & troubleshooting              │
    │   • Job scheduling (cron, systemd timers, at)                    │
    │   • Software packages & repositories                            │
    │   • Hardware, OS, and filesystem failure recovery               │
    │   • Virtual Machines (libvirt)                                   │
    │   • Container engines (create & manage containers)               │
    │   • MAC with SELinux/AppArmor                                    │
    │                                                                  │
    │  ████████████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  25%  Networking  │
    │   • IPv4/IPv6 networking & hostname resolution                  │
    │   • System time synchronization (NTP/Chrony)                     │
    │   • Network monitoring & troubleshooting                        │
    │   • OpenSSH server & client configuration                       │
    │   • Packet filtering, port redirection, NAT                     │
    │   • Static routing                                               │
    │   • Bridge & bonding devices                                     │
    │   • Reverse proxies & load balancers                             │
    │                                                                  │
    │  ████████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  20%  Storage       │
    │   • LVM storage configuration & management                      │
    │   • Virtual File System (VFS) management                         │
    │   • Filesystem creation, management & troubleshooting           │
    │   • Remote filesystems & network block devices (NFS, iSCSI)     │
    │   • Swap space configuration & management                       │
    │   • Filesystem automounters (autofs)                             │
    │   • Storage performance monitoring                              │
    │                                                                  │
    │  ████████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  20%  Essential     │
    │                                                       Commands   │
    │   • Basic Git operations                                        │
    │   • Create, configure & troubleshoot services                   │
    │   • Monitor system & service performance                        │
    │   • Application & service constraints                           │
    │   • Troubleshoot disk space issues                              │
    │   • Work with SSL certificates                                   │
    │                                                                  │
    │  █████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  10%  Users &       │
    │                                                       Groups     │
    │   • Create & manage local user & group accounts                 │
    │   • Personal & system-wide environment profiles                 │
    │   • User resource limits (ulimits)                              │
    │   • Configure & manage ACLs                                      │
    │   • Configure system for LDAP user/group accounts               │
    │                                                                  │
    │  Total: 100%   |   Pass: 66%   |   Duration: 2 hours            │
    │  Format: Performance-based (hands-on CLI tasks in a live VM)     │
    │  Versions: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS  |  Rocky Linux 9                   │
    │  📅 UPDATED: June 2026 — Official Linux Foundation Exam Domains  │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    LFCS vs OTHER LINUX CERTIFICATIONS — WHAT'S THE DIFFERENCE?
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │   CERTIFICATION   │ LFCS              │ RHCSA          │ LPIC-1  │
    │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │  Provider          │ Linux Foundation  │ Red Hat        │ LPI     │
    │  Focus             │ General SysAdmin  │ RHEL-specific  │ Neutral │
    │  Format            │ CLI hands-on ✅    │ CLI hands-on ✅│ Multiple│
    │                    │ (24 tasks, 2 hrs) │ (tasks, 2.5hr) │ Choice  │
    │  Distro            │ Ubuntu + Rocky    │ RHEL + CentOS  │ Any     │
    │  Cost              │ ~$395 (1 retake)  │ ~$400-500      │ ~$200   │
    │  Validity          │ 3 years           │ 3 years        │ 5 years │
    │  Pass Score        │ 66%               │ 70%            │ 50-60%  │
    │  Prerequisites     │ None              │ None           │ None    │
    │                    │                   │                │         │
    │  Key Topics (2026) │ Containers! ✅     │ Podman focus   │ Older   │
    │                    │ Podman, libvirt,   │ SELinux heavy  │ topics  │
    │                    │ ACLs, LDAP, Git    │                │         │
    │                    │                   │                │         │
    │  LFCS Advantages:  │ • Distro-flexible (Ubuntu AND Rocky)        │
    │                    │ • Tests modern tools (Git, containers)       │
    │                    │ • Performance-based = real skills           │
    │                    │ • Widely recognized in cloud/devops roles    │
    │                    │ • Same provider as CKA/CKAD/CKS (LF)        │
    │                                                                  │
    │  THIS GUIDE → LFCS (we run and manage Linux for anihpj)         │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    LFCS EXAM STRATEGY — TOP 10 MISTAKES TO AVOID
    ───────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  ❌ COMMON MISTAKES               ✅ WHAT TO DO INSTEAD          │
    │                                                                  │
    │  1. Using GUI tools (not allowed)  Use only CLI commands         │
    │                                                                  │
    │  2. Forgetting to make changes     Use systemctl enable,        │
    │     persistent after reboot        fstab, crontab properly      │
    │                                                                  │
    │  3. Not verifying your work        Always: systemctl status,    │
    │     immediately after changes      cat /etc/fstab, id, groups   │
    │                                                                  │
    │  4. Wrong file permissions         Use ls -la and stat before   │
    │     or file edited incorrectly     and after every change        │
    │                                                                  │
    │  5. SELinux/AppArmor blocking      Check: journalctl, audit.log │
    │     but not investigated           getenforce, aa-status         │
    │                                                                  │
    │  6. Editing /etc files without     Always: cp file file.bak     │
    │     a backup                       before editing critical files │
    │                                                                  │
    │  7. Not reading question twice     Keywords: "persistent",       │
    │                                    "for all users", "on boot"   │
    │                                                                  │
    │  8. Using deprecated commands      Use ip instead of ifconfig   │
    │     (ifconfig, netstat, etc.)      Use ss instead of netstat    │
    │                                                                  │
    │  9. Not testing service configs    nginx -t, apache2ctl -t,     │
    │     before reloading               sshd -t before restarting    │
    │                                                                  │
    │  10. Skipping practice labs        Hands-on > reading.          │
    │      and only reading theory       Linux Academy, KillerShell   │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    EXAM DAY SETUP — YOUR TERMINAL SHORTCUTS
    ──────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  Set these up the moment your exam terminal opens:               │
    │                                                                  │
    │  alias ll='ls -lah'                                              │
    │  alias grep='grep --color=auto'                                  │
    │  export EDITOR=vim                                               │
    │  set -o vi                  # vim key bindings in bash           │
    │                                                                  │
    │  Quick user info:                                                │
    │  id username                # uid, gid, groups                  │
    │  getent passwd username     # full user entry                    │
    │  getent group groupname     # full group entry                   │
    │                                                                  │
    │  Quick service check:                                            │
    │  systemctl is-enabled sshd  # does it start on boot?            │
    │  systemctl is-active sshd   # is it running right now?          │
    │  journalctl -u sshd -n 20   # last 20 log lines                 │
    │                                                                  │
    │  Quick network check:                                            │
    │  ip addr show               # all interfaces + IPs              │
    │  ss -tulnp                  # all listening ports + processes    │
    │  ip route show              # routing table                      │
    │                                                                  │
    │  Quick disk check:                                               │
    │  lsblk -f                   # all disks + filesystems            │
    │  df -hT                     # disk usage + filesystem type       │
    │  pvs && vgs && lvs          # LVM overview                      │
    │                                                                  │
    │  ⏱️ These aliases save 15-20 minutes during the exam!            │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    LINUX DOCUMENTATION BOOKMARKS FOR LFCS
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
    [TABLE: Must-know reference pages]
    | Topic                      | Command                          | Notes                     |
    | User management            | man useradd, man passwd          | /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow  |
    | Permissions & ACL          | man chmod, man setfacl           | rwxrwxrwx notation        |
    | Systemd services           | man systemctl, man systemd.unit  | Unit file syntax          |
    | Partition & Filesystems    | man fdisk, man mkfs              | GPT vs MBR                |
    | LVM                        | man pvcreate, man lvcreate       | Physical, Volume, Logical |
    | Networking                 | man ip, man nmcli                | ip addr, ip route         |
    | Firewall                   | man firewall-cmd, man ufw        | Zones, rules              |
    | SSH                        | man sshd_config, man ssh-keygen  | Daemon config file        |
    | Cron                       | man crontab, man anacron         | * * * * * syntax          |
    | SELinux                    | man semanage, man restorecon     | Contexts, policies        |

    LEARNING PATH OVERVIEW
    ──────────────────────
    [ROADMAP ILLUSTRATION: 11-Part journey from zero to LFCS certified]

    Prerequisites:
      • Basic familiarity with computers and file systems
      • Curiosity and willingness to use the terminal
      • A computer with 4 GB+ RAM (8 GB recommended for VM practice)
      • VirtualBox or VMware with Ubuntu 22.04 LTS installed
      [ICON ROW: Terminal icon | Ubuntu logo | VirtualBox icon | RAM chip]

    How to Use This Guide:
      • Each chapter builds on the previous one
      • Every concept includes anihpj/jobpost real-world server examples
      • 🟢 = Beginner | 🟡 = Intermediate | 🔴 = Advanced
      • 🎯 = LFCS Exam Topic (directly tested)
      • Commands shown as: $ user-command   |   # root-command

    [ANIHPJ SERVER JOURNEY — how this project maps to LFCS skills]
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  GOAL: Deploy anihpj (Django) + PostgreSQL on a bare Linux server  │
    │                                                                    │
    │  Part 1 → Understand the OS you'll work on                        │
    │  Part 2 → Navigate the server filesystem like a pro              │
    │  Part 3 → Automate with scripts (deploy, backup, etc.)           │
    │  Part 4 → Create the 'anihpj' service user (least privilege)     │
    │  Part 5 → Keep Gunicorn and Celery running as systemd services   │
    │  Part 6 → Configure server networking, open port 80/443          │
    │  Part 7 → Mount the PostgreSQL data volume, LVM for growth       │
    │  Part 8 → Nginx reverse proxy → Gunicorn, PostgreSQL, SSH access │
    │  Part 9 → Harden the server: firewall, SSH keys, SELinux         │
    │  Part 10 → Monitor CPU/RAM/disk, alert on issues                 │
    │  Part 11 → Cron for DB backups, full anihpj production setup     │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


    🚢 HOW LINUX, DOCKER & KUBERNETES WORK TOGETHER — THE SHIPPING PORT ANALOGY
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                     │
    │  Think of modern software deployment like a GLOBAL SHIPPING PORT:   │
    │                                                                     │
    │  🏗️ LINUX = THE PORT INFRASTRUCTURE (THE GROUND EVERYTHING SITS ON) │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │  │  The port's roads, power grid, water pipes, and foundations.  │  │
    │  │  Without it, NOTHING works — ships have nowhere to dock.      │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  🔑 Linux provides:                                            │  │
    │  │    • Process management (the workers & machinery)              │  │
    │  │    • Filesystem (the warehouse shelves & storage yards)        │  │
    │  │    • Networking (the roads & shipping lanes connecting docks)  │  │
    │  │    • Security/permissions (the security gates & ID badges)     │  │
    │  │    • User management (the port staff directory)                │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  This ENTIRE GUIDE (LFCS) = Learning to run the PORT itself    │  │
    │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    │                              │                                        │
    │                              ▼                                        │
    │  📦 DOCKER = STANDARDIZED SHIPPING CONTAINERS (ISO CONTAINERS)       │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │  │  Just like shipping containers revolutionized global trade by  │  │
    │  │  standardizing sizes (20ft, 40ft), Docker containers           │  │
    │  │  standardize how applications are packaged.                    │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  BEFORE CONTAINERS:                    WITH DOCKER CONTAINERS:  │  │
    │  │  ┌──────────────────┐                 ┌──────────────────┐     │  │
    │  │  │ Every app shipped │                 │ Standard container│     │  │
    │  │  │ differently —     │                 │ format — every app│     │  │
    │  │  │ custom crates,    │                 │ fits the same     │     │  │
    │  │  │ different sizes,  │                 │ "container ship", │     │  │
    │  │  │ fragile, messy    │                 │ "crane", "truck"  │     │  │
    │  │  └──────────────────┘                 └──────────────────┘     │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  🔑 Docker provides (Appendix H):                               │  │
    │  │    • Dockerfile = container specification sheet                │  │
    │  │    • Image = the loaded container, ready to ship               │  │
    │  │    • Container = the running instance (ship at sea)            │  │
    │  │    • Registry = container storage yard (Docker Hub)            │  │
    │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    │                              │                                        │
    │                              ▼                                        │
    │  ☸️ KUBERNETES = THE HARBOR MASTER & AUTOMATED CRANE SYSTEM          │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │  │  When you have THOUSANDS of containers moving through the      │  │
    │  │  port daily, you need automation. The harbor master (K8s)      │  │
    │  │  decides which ship goes to which berth, manages the fleet,    │  │
    │  │  replaces damaged ships, and ensures cargo arrives on time.    │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  🔑 Kubernetes provides (Appendix I):                           │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
    │  │  │ 🧭 etcd = THE HARBOR LOGBOOK                             │   │  │
    │  │  │   A distributed key-value database that records the      │   │  │
    │  │  │   EXACT state of every ship, container, and berth.       │   │  │
    │  │  │   "Source of Truth" — if it's not in the logbook,        │   │  │
    │  │  │   it doesn't exist in the port.                          │   │  │
    │  │  │   LFCS parallel: understanding etcd ≈ understanding      │   │  │
    │  │  │   /etc/passwd, /etc/fstab — configuration databases      │   │  │
    │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
    │  │  │ 📋 API Server (kube-apiserver) = THE FRONT DESK           │   │  │
    │  │  │   Every command, every query, every status update goes    │   │  │
    │  │  │   through this single entry point. You talk to the        │   │  │
    │  │  │   front desk; the front desk coordinates everything.      │   │  │
    │  │  │   LFCS parallel: kubectl ≈ SSH + systemctl combined      │   │  │
    │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
    │  │  │ 🧭 Scheduler (kube-scheduler) = THE DOCK MASTER           │   │  │
    │  │  │   "Container ship anihpj:v2 needs to dock. Which berth   │   │  │
    │  │  │   (worker node) has enough space, power, and resources?"  │   │  │
    │  │  │   The dock master evaluates ALL berths and picks the      │   │  │
    │  │  │   best one based on available capacity.                   │   │  │
    │  │  │   LFCS parallel: scheduler ≈ Linux kernel's CPU scheduler │   │  │
    │  │  │   (decides which process runs on which CPU core)          │   │  │
    │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
    │  │  │ 👷 kubelet = THE DOCK WORKER AT EACH BERTH                │   │  │
    │  │  │   Each berth (worker node) has a dock worker who:         │   │  │
    │  │  │   • Receives orders from the harbor master                │   │  │
    │  │  │   • Ensures containers are running and healthy            │   │  │
    │  │  │   • Reports status back: "Ship #3 is damaged!"            │   │  │
    │  │  │   LFCS parallel: kubelet ≈ systemd (manages services)     │   │  │
    │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
    │  │  │ 🚢 Pod = THE CONTAINER SHIP (carrying Docker containers)  │   │  │
    │  │  │   A pod is like a cargo ship that carries one or more     │   │  │
    │  │  │   Docker containers. All containers on the same ship      │   │  │
    │  │  │   share the ship's network (IP) and storage deck.         │   │  │
    │  │  │   The anihpj pod: [Django container] — a simple ship      │   │  │
    │  │  │   carrying one container. May add [Nginx sidecar] later.  │   │  │
    │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
    │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    │                                                                     │
    │  🎯 THE FULL JOURNEY OF AN ANIHPJ REQUEST — END TO END              │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  ① User visits https://anihpj.com/jobs/                       │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ② DNS resolves → Server IP (Linux networking, Ch 18-20)      │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ③ UFW Firewall checks: port 443 allowed? (Ch 21)             │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ④ Nginx receives HTTPS request (Ch 27)                       │  │
    │  │     │ SSL termination (Ch 33), static files served directly    │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ⑤ Nginx proxies to Gunicorn via Unix socket (Ch 27)          │  │
    │  │     │ /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock → file permissions matter!      │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ⑥ Gunicorn worker processes request (Ch 15-16)               │  │
    │  │     │ Running as 'anihpj' user (least privilege, Ch 12-13)    │  │
    │  │     │ Managed by systemd (auto-restart on crash, Ch 16)       │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ⑦ Django queries PostgreSQL (Ch 28)                          │  │
    │  │     │ localhost:5432, auth via pg_hba.conf                     │  │
    │  │     │ Data on LVM volume (Ch 24) for easy expansion            │  │
    │  │     │                                                           │  │
    │  │  ⑧ Django renders HTML → Gunicorn → Nginx → User's browser    │  │
    │  │                                                                │  │
    │  │  🔑 Every step in this flow is an LFCS exam topic!             │  │
    │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    [STACK LAYER DIAGRAM — from hardware to user]
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                     │
    │  🌐 USERS: https://anihpj.com/jobs/                                 │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
    │  ☸️ KUBERNETES LAYER (Orchestration)                                 │
    │     etcd → API Server → Scheduler → Controller → kubelet           │
    │     Pods: [anihpj] [anihpj] [anihpj]  (replicas=3)                │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
    │  📦 DOCKER LAYER (Containerization)                                  │
    │     Docker Engine → Images → Containers → Registry                 │
    │     Container: Django+Gunicorn, isolated, reproducible              │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
    │  🐧 LINUX LAYER (Operating System — LFCS FOCUS)                     │
    │     systemd (services) → sshd → nginx → cron → logrotate          │
    │     Filesystem (ext4/xfs) → LVM → Netplan → iptables/ufw          │
    │     Users & Groups → Permissions → PAM → SELinux/AppArmor          │
    │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
    │  💻 HARDWARE LAYER                                                  │
    │     CPU → RAM → SSD/NVMe → Network Card → Power Supply             │
    │                                                                     │
    │  🎯 LFCS covers the LINUX LAYER — the foundation everything         │
    │     else depends on. Master Linux, and you master the server.       │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 1: What is Linux? — History, Distributions & Why It Matters
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    1.1  The Problem That Created Linux
         - 1991: Linus Torvalds, a Finnish student at the University of Helsinki, starts Linux
         - Why: UNIX was expensive and proprietary; students couldn't afford it
         - GNU Project (1984): Richard Stallman creates free software tools — but no kernel
         - Linux kernel fills the gap: GNU tools + Linux kernel = complete, free OS
         - Linux = Linux kernel + GNU tools = a free, open-source UNIX-like OS
         - Today: powers 96% of the world's top 1 million web servers
         - Android phones, AWS EC2, Google Cloud, Azure VMs — all run Linux
         - Your anihpj server runs on Linux — understanding it is non-negotiable
         [TIMELINE DIAGRAM: 1969 UNIX → 1984 GNU → 1991 Linux kernel → 2003 CentOS
          → 2004 Ubuntu → 2014 Kubernetes runs on Linux → Today]

         [LINUX HISTORY TIMELINE — key moments]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1969 ── AT&T Bell Labs creates UNIX (Thompson & Ritchie)        │
         │  1983 ── GNU Project started (Richard Stallman, MIT)             │
         │  1991 ── Linux kernel v0.01 by Linus Torvalds (10,239 lines!)    │
         │  1992 ── Linux + GNU tools = first complete free OS              │
         │  1994 ── Linux kernel 1.0 officially released                    │
         │  1996 ── Tux the penguin becomes the Linux mascot                │
         │  2004 ── Ubuntu 4.10 released (first Ubuntu LTS)                 │
         │  2007 ── Android launched — Linux kernel inside every phone      │
         │  2014 ── Kubernetes open-sourced by Google (runs on Linux)       │
         │  2022 ── Ubuntu 22.04 LTS "Jammy Jellyfish" ← THIS GUIDE        │
         │  Today─ 96% of top web servers + 100% of supercomputers = Linux  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [LINUX USAGE TODAY — where it dominates]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Web Servers       ████████████████████████████████  96%         │
         │  Top 500 Supercomp ████████████████████████████████ 100%         │
         │  Cloud (AWS/GCP)   ████████████████████████████████ ~90%         │
         │  Android phones    ████████████████████████████████ ~70% market  │
         │  Desktop PCs       ██░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░   ~3%         │
         │                                                                  │
         │  Why wins on servers? Free + stable + SSH-able + scriptable      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE LINUX FAMILY TREE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │   🧬 UNIX (1969)                                                │
         │       │                                                          │
         │       ├──── BSD (Berkeley) ──── macOS (Darwin) ──── iOS         │
         │       │                                                          │
         │       └──── GNU/Linux (1991) ──── DISTRIBUTIONS:                │
         │                 │                                                │
         │                 ├── 🔴 Red Hat ── CentOS, Rocky, Fedora, RHEL   │
         │                 │                                                │
         │                 ├── 🟠 Debian ─── Ubuntu 22.04 ← THIS GUIDE    │
         │                 │                  ├── Linux Mint               │
         │                 │                  └── Raspberry Pi OS          │
         │                 │                                                │
         │                 └── 🟢 Arch ──── Arch Linux, Manjaro            │
         │                                                                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.2  Linux vs Windows vs macOS — What Makes Linux Different
         - Open source: anyone can read, modify, distribute the code freely
         - Multi-user: designed from the ground up for many users simultaneously
         - Everything is a file: devices, processes, network sockets = files
         - Case-sensitive: "anihpj" ≠ "Anihpj" ≠ "ANIHPJ" — no exceptions
         - No drive letters: one unified tree starting at "/" (not C:\, D:\)
         - Package managers: install software with one command (apt, yum, dnf)
         - SSH-native: designed for remote administration from Day 1
         - Free: no per-server license — critical for scaling anihpj infrastructure
         [COMPARISON TABLE: Linux vs Windows vs macOS — key admin differences]

         [LINUX vs WINDOWS vs macOS — ADMIN COMPARISON]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                   LINUX           WINDOWS        macOS           │
         │                                                                  │
         │  Cost            Free (mostly)   $99-200/server Free w/ Mac     │
         │  Source Code     Open Source     Proprietary    Partial open    │
         │  Remote Admin    SSH (native)    RDP / WinRM    SSH (native)    │
         │  Case Sensitive  YES ✅          NO ❌           Usually NO      │
         │  Filesystem Root  /              C:\, D:\        /              │
         │  Package Manager apt, dnf, etc.  MS Store       Homebrew       │
         │  Server Market   96% of web      IIS-based       Rarely servers │
         │  Customizable    Completely      Limited         Moderate       │
         │  anihpj runs on  Native ✅       WSL2 workaround Yes            │
         │  LFCS exam uses  Ubuntu + Rocky  —               —              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE "EVERYTHING IS A FILE" CONCEPT]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  In Linux, EVERYTHING is represented as a file:                  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  /dev/sda          → your hard drive (block device file)        │
         │  /dev/null         → a black hole (discard output forever)      │
         │  /dev/random       → random number generator device             │
         │  /proc/cpuinfo     → your CPU info (virtual kernel file)        │
         │  /proc/1234/       → all info about process with PID 1234       │
         │  /proc/net/tcp     → active TCP connections (virtual file)      │
         │  /sys/class/net/   → your network interfaces as files           │
         │                                                                  │
         │  This unification means: cat, grep, echo, awk work on ALL of   │
         │  these. Same tool, hardware OR process OR network OR config.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.3  Linux Distributions — Choosing the Right One
         - "Distribution" (distro) = Linux kernel + software + package manager
         - Ubuntu 22.04 LTS: best for beginners, LFCS exam-ready, 5-year support
         - Rocky Linux 9: enterprise, CentOS replacement, RHEL-compatible
         - Debian: ultra-stable, parent of Ubuntu
         - For anihpj server: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS is our choice
         [DISTRO COMPARISON CARD: Ubuntu vs CentOS/Rocky vs Debian — when to use]

         [DISTRO QUICK REFERENCE — package managers]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DISTRO FAMILY     │ PACKAGE MANAGER  │ INSTALL COMMAND          │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  Ubuntu / Debian   │ APT (dpkg)       │ apt install nginx         │
         │  RHEL/Rocky/CentOS │ DNF / YUM (rpm)  │ dnf install nginx         │
         │  Arch Linux        │ pacman           │ pacman -S nginx           │
         │  SUSE / openSUSE   │ zypper (rpm)     │ zypper install nginx      │
         │                                                                  │
         │  THIS GUIDE uses: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS (apt)                        │
         │  LFCS also tests: Rocky Linux 9 (dnf)                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WHICH DISTRO SHOULD I USE? — DECISION TREE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  START: What's your goal?                                        │
         │  │                                                               │
         │  ├── 🎓 I'm studying for LFCS exam                               │
         │  │   ├── Primary practice VM? → Ubuntu 22.04 LTS (apt)          │
         │  │   │   • Most LFCS tasks tested on Ubuntu                     │
         │  │   │   • Huge community, tutorials everywhere                 │
         │  │   │   • 5 years of free security updates                     │
         │  │   │                                                           │
         │  │   └── Second practice VM? → Rocky Linux 9 (dnf)              │
         │  │       • LFCS exam has BOTH distros!                          │
         │  │       • Practice firewalld, SELinux (not ufw, AppArmor)      │
         │  │       • Enterprise RHEL compatibility                        │
         │  │                                                               │
         │  ├── 🏢 Production servers (enterprise)                          │
         │  │   ├── Need RHEL support contract? → RHEL 9 (paid)            │
         │  │   └── Want free RHEL-compatible? → Rocky Linux 9 / AlmaLinux │
         │  │                                                               │
         │  ├── 🏠 Personal laptop/desktop?                                 │
         │  │   ├── First-time Linux user? → Ubuntu Desktop / Linux Mint   │
         │  │   ├── Developer (cutting-edge)? → Fedora Workstation         │
         │  │   └── Power user (full control)? → Arch Linux                │
         │  │                                                               │
         │  ├── 🖥️ Server (minimal, ultra-stable)                           │
         │  │   └── Debian Stable (rock-solid, slower updates)              │
         │  │                                                               │
         │  └── 📱 Small/Embedded/IoT?                                      │
         │      └── Raspberry Pi OS / Alpine Linux (tiny, musl libc)        │
         │                                                                  │
         │  🎯 FOR LFCS: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS + Rocky Linux 9 = 2 VMs          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.4  The Linux Kernel — The Heart of the OS  🎯
         - Kernel = the core program that talks directly to hardware
         - It manages: CPU time scheduling, RAM allocation, I/O, filesystem, networking
         - User space vs kernel space — separation for stability and security
         - Kernel ring 0 (most privileged) vs ring 3 (user programs)
         - How to check your kernel version: uname -r
         - uname -a → all info: kernel version, hostname, arch, date
         - Kernel modules: dynamically loadable drivers — no reboot needed
         - lsmod → list loaded modules | modprobe → load | rmmod → unload
         - /proc/version → kernel version as readable file
         - Kernel parameters tunable at runtime with sysctl (without rebooting!)
         [DIAGRAM: Hardware ← Kernel ← System Calls ← User Programs]

         [KERNEL COMMANDS QUICK REFERENCE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  COMMAND               │ WHAT IT SHOWS / DOES                   │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  uname -r              │ Just the kernel version: 6.5.0-35-gen  │
         │  uname -a              │ All: version, hostname, arch, date      │
         │  uname -m              │ Machine architecture: x86_64 / aarch64  │
         │  cat /proc/version     │ Kernel version + GCC build info         │
         │  lsmod                 │ All currently loaded kernel modules     │
         │  modprobe modulename   │ Load a kernel module (with dependencies)│
         │  modinfo modulename    │ Description, author, params of module   │
         │  rmmod modulename      │ Unload a kernel module                  │
         │  sysctl -a             │ ALL kernel runtime parameters           │
         │  sysctl kernel.hostname│ Read one specific parameter             │
         │  sysctl -w vm.swappiness=10 → Set parameter (temporary)         │
         │  dmesg | tail -20      │ Last 20 kernel boot/hardware messages   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [KERNEL LAYERS DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  USER SPACE (programs you run)                                   │
         │  ┌──────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ bash │ │  Python  │ │ nginx  │ │  anihpj (Django app) │    │
         │  └──────┘ └──────────┘ └────────┘ └──────────────────────┘    │
         │       │         │           │                │                  │
         │       └─────────┴───────────┴────────────────┘                  │
         │                           │                                      │
         │                     System Calls                                 │
         │                    (read, write, open, fork)                     │
         │                           │                                      │
         │  KERNEL SPACE (protected core)                                   │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │  Process Mgmt │ Memory Mgmt │ Filesystem │  Networking  │    │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                           │                                      │
         │  HARDWARE                                                        │
         │  ┌──────┐  ┌─────┐  ┌──────┐  ┌────────┐  ┌─────────────┐    │
         │  │ CPU  │  │ RAM │  │ Disk │  │ NIC    │  │ USB/PCI Dev │    │
         │  └──────┘  └─────┘  └──────┘  └────────┘  └─────────────┘    │
         │                                                                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.5  Installing Ubuntu 22.04 — Your Practice Lab
         - Download Ubuntu 22.04 LTS ISO from ubuntu.com/download/server
         - Install VirtualBox (free) or VMware Workstation Player
         - Create a VM: 2 vCPU, 4-8 GB RAM, 50 GB primary disk
         - Add a SECOND 10 GB disk (essential for LVM and partition labs)
         - Choose "Ubuntu Server" (no GUI) — never "Ubuntu Desktop" for exams
         - During partitioning: choose "Use entire disk and set up LVM"
         - Enable OpenSSH server during install → SSH access enabled
         - Also set up Rocky Linux 9 in a second VM for dual-distro practice
         - Take a VM snapshot immediately after install (your clean restore point)
         [STEP-BY-STEP INSTALL CARDS with screenshots descriptions]

         [VM SETUP CHECKLIST FOR LFCS PRACTICE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ☐ Download Ubuntu 22.04 LTS server ISO (ubuntu.com/download)   │
         │  ☐ Create VM: 2 vCPU, 4-8 GB RAM, 50 GB primary disk           │
         │  ☐ Add a second 10 GB disk (for LVM/partition labs)             │
         │  ☐ Enable NAT network (internet → apt install works)            │
         │  ☐ Enable Host-Only adapter (SSH from your laptop)              │
         │  ☐ Install Ubuntu Server (NOT Desktop — no GUI needed)          │
         │  ☐ During partitioning: choose LVM option                       │
         │  ☐ Install OpenSSH server when prompted                         │
         │  ☐ Snapshot the VM after install ("clean state")                │
         │  ☐ Rocky Linux 9 second VM (dnf, firewalld, SELinux practice)  │
         │  ☐ Test: ssh your-user@vm-ip-address from your laptop           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.6  The LFCS Exam Environment — What to Expect  🎯
         - Two VMs provided: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS + Rocky Linux 9
         - Browser-based terminal interface (no copy-paste from outside)
         - All tasks evaluated automatically by checking the system STATE
         - You CAN use: man pages, --help, /usr/share/doc/, tab completion
         - You CANNOT use: internet, Google, external notes, AI tools
         - Time: 2 hours — ~24 performance-based (hands-on CLI) tasks
         - "Persistent" keyword = change must survive reboot (/etc/fstab, /etc/systemd/)
         - Partial credit: some tasks award points for partial completion
         [EXAM ENVIRONMENT DIAGRAM: Browser → VM terminal]

         [LFCS EXAM FORMAT AT A GLANCE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ⏱️  Duration:    2 hours                                         │
         │  📋  Tasks:       ~24 performance-based (hands-on CLI)           │
         │  ✅  Pass score:  66% (approx. 16 of 24 correct)                 │
         │  🖥️  Environment: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS + Rocky Linux 9 VMs           │
         │  📖  Open book:   man pages, --help only (NO internet)           │
         │  🔄  Retake:      1 free retake included with registration        │
         │  📅  Validity:    3 years from pass date                         │
         │  🎓  Provider:    Linux Foundation (same as CKA, CKAD, CKS)     │
         │  💰  Cost:        ~$395 USD (includes 1 retake)                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HOW EXAM TASKS ARE GRADED — key insight]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Tasks describe OUTCOMES, not commands. Grading checks state:    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  Task: "Create user 'alice' with UID 2001, add to group 'devs'" │
         │  Graded by: grep alice /etc/passwd | grep 2001 → pass/fail      │
         │                                                                  │
         │  Task: "Mount /dev/sdb1 at /data persistently"                  │
         │  Graded by: reboot → is /data mounted? /etc/fstab entry exists? │
         │                                                                  │
         │  KEY INSIGHT: The SYSTEM STATE is what matters.                  │
         │  → Any valid command that reaches the right result = credit      │
         │  → "Persistent" = must survive a reboot (check /etc/fstab)      │
         │  → Test your work: verify BEFORE moving on                       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 2: The Terminal — Your Primary Tool
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    2.1  Why the Terminal? GUI vs CLI  🎯
         - GUI (Graphical User Interface): click, point, drag — intuitive but slow, not scriptable
         - CLI (Command Line Interface): type commands — fast, scriptable, SSH-friendly
         - Remote servers have NO GUI — the terminal is your ONLY window into the machine
         - SSH into your anihpj server = terminal only, no exceptions, always
         - Scripting: CLI commands can be combined into shell scripts — automation at scale
         - Speed: a sequence of 5 GUI clicks = 1 typed command
         - Reproducibility: CLI commands are copy-paste repeatable; GUI clicks are not
         - Linux sysadmins LIVE in the terminal — 100% of LFCS exam is CLI
         [ANALOGY CARD: GUI = automatic car | CLI = manual car — more control]

         [GUI vs CLI — COMPARISON FOR anihpj ADMIN]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TASK                  │ GUI ACTION         │ CLI COMMAND        │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  Create a directory    │ Right-click → New  │ mkdir /var/www/app │
         │  Read a config file    │ Double-click, text │ cat /etc/nginx.conf│
         │  Restart a service     │ Find in menu...    │ systemctl restart  │
         │  Check disk usage      │ Properties → disk  │ df -h              │
         │  Search file contents  │ Open search tool   │ grep "error" *.log │
         │  Remote administration │ VPN + RDP (heavy)  │ ssh user@server    │
         │  Automate 100 servers  │ Impossible         │ for i in servers...│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE TERMINAL ANATOMY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  owner@ubuntu-server:~$  ls -la /var/www/anihpj                 │
         │  │     │              │  │                                       │
         │  │     │              │  └─ The command you type                │
         │  │     │              └──── Shell prompt ($ = user, # = root)  │
         │  │     └─────────────────── Current directory (~ = home)        │
         │  └───────────────────────── username@hostname                    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  total 48                                                        │
         │  drwxr-xr-x  6 anihpj anihpj 4096 May 24 18:00 .              │
         │  drwxr-xr-x  3 root   root   4096 May 24 17:00 ..             │
         │  -rw-r--r--  1 anihpj anihpj 1204 May 24 18:00 manage.py      │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────── output ─────  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.2  Bash — The Default Shell  🎯
         - Shell = the program that interprets and executes your typed commands
         - Bash (Bourne Again SHell): the default shell on Ubuntu, Rocky Linux, most distros
         - Other shells: zsh (macOS default, more features), fish (friendly), sh (POSIX minimal)
         - Check your shell: echo $SHELL → /bin/bash
         - Change default shell: chsh -s /bin/zsh (change shell)
         - The shell reads startup files on login: /etc/profile → ~/.bash_profile → ~/.bashrc
         - ~/.bashrc: customizations for interactive non-login shells (aliases, PATH, PS1)
         - ~/.bash_profile: runs on login shells (SSH sessions, tty logins)
         - Tab completion: press Tab once (complete) or twice (show options)
         - Command history: stored in ~/.bash_history (~500 recent commands)
         [DIAGRAM: You type → Shell parses → Kernel executes → Output returns to Shell → You see it]

         [BASH STARTUP FILE LOADING ORDER]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LOGIN SHELL (ssh user@host or tty):                            │
         │  /etc/profile  →  ~/.bash_profile (or ~/.profile)               │
         │                                                                  │
         │  INTERACTIVE NON-LOGIN (new terminal tab, bash command):        │
         │  /etc/bash.bashrc  →  ~/.bashrc                                  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  WHAT TO PUT WHERE:                                              │
         │  ~/.bashrc     → aliases, PS1 prompt, custom functions          │
         │  ~/.bash_profile → PATH additions, environment variables        │
         │                                                                  │
         │  Useful: add to ~/.bashrc for anihpj work:                      │
         │    alias ll='ls -la'                                             │
         │    alias cdw='cd /var/www/anihpj'                               │
         │    export DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE=anihpj.settings.production     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.3  Essential Terminal Navigation  🎯
         - pwd              Print Working Directory — where am I right now?
         - ls               List directory contents (alphabetical)
         - ls -la           Long format, show hidden files (files starting with .)
         - ls -lh           Long format with human-readable file sizes (KB, MB, GB)
         - ls -lt           Sort by modification time (newest first)
         - cd               Change Directory (cd .., cd ~, cd -, cd /var/log)
         - cd -             Go back to PREVIOUS directory (very useful!)
         - tree             Show directory tree visually (install: apt install tree)
         - clear            Clear the terminal screen (or Ctrl+L)
         - history          See your last 500 commands
         - !!               Repeat last command (sudo !!)
         - !string          Repeat last command starting with 'string'
         - !123             Repeat command #123 from history
         [HANDS-ON BOX: Navigate to the anihpj project directory structure]

         [DIRECTORY NAVIGATION QUICK REFERENCE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  WHERE I AM      │  COMMAND             │  WHERE I GO            │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  /home/owner     │  cd /                │  / (filesystem root)   │
         │  /home/owner     │  cd ..               │  /home (parent dir)    │
         │  /var/log        │  cd ~                │  /home/owner (home)    │
         │  /etc/nginx      │  cd -                │  previous directory    │
         │  anywhere        │  cd /var/www/anihpj  │  absolute path         │
         │  /var/www        │  cd anihpj           │  relative path         │
         │  /home/owner     │  cd ../..            │  two levels up         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ls OUTPUT EXPLAINED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ ls -la /var/www/anihpj                                        │
         │  total 48                                                        │
         │  drwxr-xr-x  6 anihpj www-data 4096 May 24 18:00 .             │
         │  drwxr-xr-x  3 root   root     4096 May 24 17:00 ..            │
         │  -rw-r--r--  1 anihpj anihpj   1204 May 24 18:00 manage.py     │
         │  │              │     │         │    │              └── filename  │
         │  │              │     │         │    └────────────── date/time   │
         │  │              │     │         └─────────────────── file size   │
         │  │              │     └───────────────────────────── group       │
         │  │              └─────────────────────────────────── owner       │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────── permissions │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.4  Getting Help — man, --help, info, apropos
         - man command     Full manual page (press q to quit, / to search)
         - command --help  Quick summary of options
         - man -k keyword  Search all man pages for a keyword (= apropos)
         - info command    More detailed info (GNU-style docs)
         - /usr/share/doc/ Package-installed documentation files
         [FLOW CHART: Don't know a command? → man page → --help → apropos]

         [MAN PAGE SECTIONS — what the numbers mean]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  man 1 ls      →  Section 1: User commands (most common)        │
         │  man 2 open    →  Section 2: System calls (C programming)       │
         │  man 3 printf  →  Section 3: Library functions                  │
         │  man 5 fstab   →  Section 5: File formats (/etc/fstab, etc.)   │
         │  man 7 signal  →  Section 7: Overviews and special topics       │
         │  man 8 fdisk   →  Section 8: System administration commands     │
         │                                                                  │
         │  💡 man 5 fstab  is CRUCIAL for the LFCS exam!                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.5  Terminal Keyboard Shortcuts  🎯
         - Ctrl+C    Kill the running process (stop it immediately) — most used
         - Ctrl+Z    Suspend (pause) the running process → use bg/fg to resume
         - Ctrl+D    End of input / logout from shell (like typing "exit")
         - Ctrl+L    Clear screen (same as "clear" command)
         - Ctrl+A    Go to beginning of line (like Home key)
         - Ctrl+E    Go to end of line (like End key)
         - Ctrl+R    Reverse search through command history (type to search)
         - Ctrl+W    Delete one word backward
         - Ctrl+U    Delete from cursor to beginning of line
         - Ctrl+K    Delete from cursor to end of line
         - Alt+F     Move forward one word
         - Alt+B     Move backward one word
         - Tab       Auto-complete filenames and commands (use constantly!)
         - ↑ / ↓     Previous / next command in history
         [CHEAT CARD: All terminal shortcuts in one box — save and print]

         [EXAM SPEED TRICK — these 5 shortcuts save minutes]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. Ctrl+R  → Reverse search history for previous commands      │
         │     (type a few letters → it finds the last matching command)    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  2. Tab Tab → See all completions (very useful for man pages)    │
         │     man sys[TAB TAB] → shows all man pages starting with sys     │
         │                                                                  │
         │  3. !! → Repeat last command (classic: sudo !!)                  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  4. Ctrl+A then Ctrl+K → Clear entire line instantly             │
         │                                                                  │
         │  5. Alt+. → Insert last argument from previous command           │
         │     mkdir /long/path/here → cd [Alt+.] = cd /long/path/here     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.6  Terminal Multiplexer — tmux & screen  🎯
         - Why: SSH sessions disconnect → running processes DIE without tmux
         - tmux: modern, recommended — split panes, windows, detach/reattach sessions
         - screen: older but universally available on all distros
         - tmux new -s anihpj         → create a named session
         - tmux attach -t anihpj      → reattach after disconnect
         - Prefix key: Ctrl+B (default) — precedes all tmux commands
         - Ctrl+B then %              → split pane vertically
         - Ctrl+B then "              → split pane horizontally
         - Ctrl+B then d              → DETACH session (leaves it running!)
         - Ctrl+B then c              → create new window
         - Ctrl+B then n/p            → next/previous window
         - Kill session: tmux kill-session -t anihpj
         [DIAGRAM: SSH disconnect without tmux vs with tmux — what survives]

         [TMUX FOR anihpj — PRACTICAL WORKFLOW]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SSH into server → start tmux → name it "deploy"                │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
         │  │  WINDOW 1: deployment        WINDOW 2: logs            │     │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐      │     │
         │  │  │ git pull         │        │ tail -f /var/log/ │      │     │
         │  │  │ pip install -r..  │        │ nginx/error.log  │      │     │
         │  │  │ python manage.py │        │ (live log view)  │      │     │
         │  │  │ migrate          │        │                  │      │     │
         │  │  └──────────────────┘        └──────────────────┘      │     │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
         │                                                                  │
         │  If SSH drops → tmux session SURVIVES on server!               │
         │  → Reconnect: ssh server → tmux attach -t deploy               │
         │  → See where deployment left off                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [TMUX QUICK REFERENCE CARD]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  COMMAND / SHORTCUT         │  WHAT IT DOES                     │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  tmux new -s name           │  Create named session             │
         │  tmux ls                    │  List all sessions                │
         │  tmux attach -t name        │  Attach to existing session       │
         │  tmux kill-session -t name  │  Kill a session                   │
         │  Ctrl+B d                   │  Detach (session keeps running)   │
         │  Ctrl+B %                   │  Split pane vertically            │
         │  Ctrl+B "                   │  Split pane horizontally          │
         │  Ctrl+B arrow keys          │  Move between panes               │
         │  Ctrl+B c                   │  Create new window                │
         │  Ctrl+B n / p               │  Next / previous window           │
         │  Ctrl+B &                   │  Kill current window              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 3: The Linux Filesystem Hierarchy
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    3.1  The Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS)
         - Linux follows a standard: every directory has a defined purpose
         - Everything starts at "/" (root) — the single root of the entire tree
         - No drive letters (no C:\) — /dev/sda1 is mounted INTO the tree
         - Understanding FHS = knowing WHERE to find (and put) things
         [TREE DIAGRAM: Full filesystem hierarchy from / down]

         [THE LINUX FILESYSTEM TREE — complete map]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  /                      ← ROOT (the beginning of everything)    │
         │  ├── bin  → /usr/bin    ← Essential user commands (ls, cp, mv)  │
         │  ├── boot               ← Bootloader, kernel images             │
         │  ├── dev                ← Device files (/dev/sda, /dev/null)    │
         │  ├── etc                ← ALL system configuration files ← KEY  │
         │  │   ├── nginx/         ← Nginx config                          │
         │  │   ├── systemd/       ← Service definitions                   │
         │  │   ├── ssh/           ← SSH daemon config                     │
         │  │   └── passwd, fstab  ← Users, mounts                        │
         │  ├── home               ← User home directories                 │
         │  │   └── owner/         ← Your files are here                  │
         │  ├── lib  → /usr/lib    ← Shared libraries (.so files)          │
         │  ├── media              ← Auto-mounted removable media           │
         │  ├── mnt                ← Manual mount point                    │
         │  ├── opt                ← Optional/third-party software         │
         │  ├── proc               ← Virtual: process & kernel info        │
         │  ├── root               ← root user's home directory            │
         │  ├── run                ← Runtime data (PIDs, sockets)          │
         │  ├── sbin → /usr/sbin   ← System admin commands (fdisk, etc.)   │
         │  ├── srv                ← Service data (web files, FTP files)   │
         │  ├── sys                ← Virtual: kernel/hardware info         │
         │  ├── tmp                ← Temporary files (cleared on reboot)   │
         │  ├── usr                ← User programs and data (read-only)    │
         │  │   ├── bin/           ← All user commands                     │
         │  │   ├── local/         ← Locally compiled software             │
         │  │   └── share/         ← Documentation, man pages              │
         │  └── var                ← Variable data (logs, databases, mail) │
         │      ├── log/           ← System log files ← KEY for LFCS      │
         │      ├── www/           ← Web server document roots            │
         │      └── lib/           ← Application state data               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.2  Where anihpj Lives on the Server
         - /var/www/anihpj/        → Application source code
         - /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj  → Nginx virtual host config
         - /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service → Systemd service unit
         - /var/log/anihpj/        → Application logs
         - /etc/postgresql/        → PostgreSQL configuration
         - /home/anihpj/           → Service user home (if using dedicated user)
         [DEPLOYMENT MAP CARD: Where every anihpj file lives]

    3.3  Absolute vs Relative Paths  🎯
         - Absolute: starts with / — full path from root → /var/www/anihpj
         - Relative: starts from CURRENT directory → ../anihpj (up, then in)
         - Special: .  = current directory | ..  = parent | ~  = your home dir
         - Absolute paths always work regardless of where you are
         - Relative paths depend on your current location (pwd)
         - When to use absolute: scripts, cron jobs, systemd services
         - When to use relative: interactive navigation (faster to type)
         [VISUAL: Two trees showing absolute vs relative path resolution]

         [ABSOLUTE vs RELATIVE — SIDE BY SIDE EXAMPLES]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Current directory: /var/www                                     │
         │                                                                  │
         │  ABSOLUTE PATHS (always start with /):                          │
         │    cd /var/www/anihpj        ← always works, anywhere           │
         │    cat /etc/nginx/nginx.conf ← always works, anywhere           │
         │    ls /home/owner/           ← always works, anywhere           │
         │                                                                  │
         │  RELATIVE PATHS (depend on current dir = /var/www):             │
         │    cd anihpj                 ← works (anihpj is IN /var/www)    │
         │    cd ../log                 ← go up to /var, then into log     │
         │    cat ./anihpj/manage.py    ← ./ = same as just anihpj/       │
         │    cd ~                      ← always goes to your home dir     │
         │                                                                  │
         │  FOR SCRIPTS: ALWAYS use absolute paths!                        │
         │  Cron, systemd don't know your "current directory"              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.4  File Types in Linux
         - Regular file (-)    → text files, binaries, images
         - Directory (d)       → a container for files
         - Symbolic link (l)   → a pointer/shortcut to another file
         - Block device (b)    → /dev/sda (hard disk)
         - Character device (c)→ /dev/tty (terminal), /dev/null
         - Named pipe (p)      → inter-process communication
         - Socket (s)          → network/IPC socket file
         [TABLE: All file types with ls -la output examples]

         [FILE TYPE IDENTIFICATION — reading ls -la output]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ ls -la /var/www/anihpj                                       │
         │                                                                  │
         │  drwxr-xr-x  6 anihpj anihpj  4096 May 24 manage.py            │
         │  │                                                               │
         │  d = Directory                                                   │
         │  - = Regular file                                                │
         │  l = Symbolic link (shows: link -> target)                      │
         │  b = Block device                                                │
         │  c = Character device                                            │
         │  p = Named pipe (FIFO)                                           │
         │  s = Socket                                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.5  Hidden Files and Dotfiles
         - Files starting with . are hidden (dotfiles) — invisible to plain ls
         - ls -a or ls -la to see them
         - ~/.bashrc       → Bash config, aliases, PATH modifications
         - ~/.bash_history → Your command history (up to 1000 lines)
         - ~/.ssh/         → SSH keys and authorized_keys
         - ~/.profile      → Login shell settings
         - ~/.gitignore    → Personal Git ignore patterns
         - /etc/.htpasswd  → Web server password files
         - Hiding ≠ Security: dotfiles are still world-readable unless permissions set
         [NOTE BOX: anihpj's .env file is a dotfile — secrets hidden by default]

         [ANIHPJ DOTFILES ON THE SERVER — map]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  /var/www/anihpj/.env          → Django SECRET_KEY, DB password │
         │  /var/www/anihpj/.gitignore    → files excluded from git        │
         │  /home/anihpj/.bashrc          → service user shell config      │
         │  /home/anihpj/.ssh/            → deploy key for git pull        │
         │  /home/anihpj/.profile         → service user env vars          │
         │                                                                  │
         │  IMPORTANT: .env should be chmod 600 AND owned by the app user  │
         │    chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env                               │
         │    chown anihpj:anihpj /var/www/anihpj/.env                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 4: Linux Permissions — Understanding Who Can Do What  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    4.1  The Permission Model — Three Levels
         - Every file has an OWNER (user), a GROUP, and OTHER (everyone else)
         - Three permission bits: Read (r), Write (w), Execute (x)
         - The permission string: -rwxr-xr-- (10 characters)
         [DIAGRAM: Breaking down -rwxr-xr-- bit by bit]

         [PERMISSION STRING DECODED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │   - r w x   r - x   r - -                                       │
         │   │ │ │ │   │ │ │   │ │ │                                       │
         │   │ └─┴─┘   └─┴─┘   └─┴─┘                                       │
         │   │  OWNER   GROUP   OTHER                                       │
         │   │                                                              │
         │   └─ File type: - = regular, d = dir, l = link                  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  r = Read    (4) → view file contents / list directory          │
         │  w = Write   (2) → modify file / create files in directory      │
         │  x = Execute (1) → run as program / enter directory             │
         │  - = No permission (0)                                           │
         │                                                                  │
         │  EXAMPLE: -rwxr-xr--                                            │
         │    Owner  (rwx) = 7  → can read, write, execute                 │
         │    Group  (r-x) = 5  → can read and execute                     │
         │    Other  (r--) = 4  → can only read                            │
         │    Written as octal: 754                                         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.2  chmod — Changing Permissions  🎯
         - Symbolic mode: chmod u+x file  (add execute for user)
         - Octal mode:    chmod 755 file  (owner=rwx, group=rx, other=rx)
         - Recursive:     chmod -R 644 /var/www/anihpj/
         - Common patterns: 644 (files), 755 (dirs/scripts), 600 (private keys)
         [REFERENCE TABLE: chmod common permission patterns with use cases]

         [CHMOD QUICK REFERENCE — most used permission sets]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PERMISSION  │ OCTAL │ USE CASE                                  │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  -rw-------  │  600  │ Private SSH key (~/.ssh/id_rsa)           │
         │  -rw-r--r--  │  644  │ Config files, static HTML, .env files    │
         │  -rwxr-xr-x  │  755  │ Scripts, directories, executables        │
         │  -rwxrwxrwx  │  777  │ ⚠️ DANGEROUS — avoid in production        │
         │  drwx------  │  700  │ Private directory (home dir)             │
         │  drwxr-xr-x  │  755  │ Public-read directory (/var/www)         │
         │  drwxrwxr-x  │  775  │ Group-writable directory                 │
         │                                                                  │
         │  For anihpj:                                                     │
         │    chmod 644 /var/www/anihpj/*.py    (source files)             │
         │    chmod 755 /var/www/anihpj/        (project directory)        │
         │    chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env    (secrets!)                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.3  chown & chgrp — Changing Ownership  🎯
         - chown user:group file         → change both owner and group
         - chown owner file              → change only the owner
         - chgrp group file              → change only the group
         - chown -R anihpj:www-data /var/www/anihpj/  → recursive ownership change
         - chown --reference=reffile file  → copy ownership from another file
         - Only root can change the owner; a user can change group to a group they belong to
         [SCENARIO: Setting correct ownership for the anihpj web app]

         [CHOWN SCENARIO — setting up anihpj correctly]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # anihpj app structure — who owns what and why                  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Static files need to be readable by nginx (www-data group)   │
         │  chown -R anihpj:www-data /var/www/anihpj/                      │
         │  chmod -R 750 /var/www/anihpj/                                  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # .env must be owned by app user ONLY (not readable by nginx)  │
         │  chown anihpj:anihpj /var/www/anihpj/.env                       │
         │  chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env                                 │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Verify ownership:                                             │
         │  ls -la /var/www/anihpj/.env                                    │
         │  # → -rw------- 1 anihpj anihpj 512 May 24 /var/www/anihpj/.env │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Add yourself to www-data group for read access:              │
         │  usermod -aG www-data owner                                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.4  Special Permission Bits — SUID, SGID, Sticky Bit  🎯
         - SUID (4xxx): run file AS its owner regardless of who calls it
           → /usr/bin/passwd is SUID root — anyone can change their password
           → ls shows 's' in owner execute position: -rwsr-xr-x
         - SGID (2xxx): run as group / new files inherit parent dir's group
           → Shared team directories: new files auto-get the team's group
           → ls shows 's' in group execute position: -rwxr-sr-x
         - Sticky bit (1xxx): only the file OWNER can delete it from the directory
           → /tmp has sticky bit: everyone writes there but only YOU delete your files
           → ls shows 't' in other execute position: drwxrwxrwt
         [REAL EXAMPLES: ls -la /usr/bin/passwd showing the 's' bit]

         [SPECIAL BITS IN PRACTICE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ ls -la /usr/bin/passwd                                        │
         │  -rwsr-xr-x 1 root root 68208 Jan 1 /usr/bin/passwd             │
         │       ^──── 's' here = SUID set (runs as root no matter who calls)│
         │                                                                  │
         │  $ ls -la /tmp                                                   │
         │  drwxrwxrwt 12 root root 4096 May 24 /tmp                       │
         │            ^── 't' here = Sticky Bit (you can't delete others' files)│
         │                                                                  │
         │  Set SUID:   chmod u+s file  OR  chmod 4755 file               │
         │  Set SGID:   chmod g+s dir   OR  chmod 2755 dir                │
         │  Set Sticky: chmod +t dir    OR  chmod 1777 /tmp               │
         │                                                                  │
         │  Find SUID files (security audit): find / -perm -4000 -type f  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.5  umask — Default Permission Mask  🎯
         - umask defines what permissions are SUBTRACTED when creating new files
         - Default umask 022 → new files get 644, new directories get 755
         - More restrictive: umask 027 → files get 640, dirs get 750
         - View current: umask (shows 0022)
         - Change temporarily: umask 027
         - Change permanently: add "umask 027" to ~/.bashrc or /etc/profile
         [DIAGRAM: umask calculation — 666 (file max) or 777 (dir max) minus mask]

         [UMASK CALCULATION TABLE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  HOW umask WORKS:                                                │
         │    New file max = 666 (files can never be executable by default) │
         │    New dir  max = 777                                            │
         │                                                                  │
         │  umask = 022:                                                    │
         │    File: 666 - 022 = 644  (-rw-r--r--)                          │
         │    Dir:  777 - 022 = 755  (drwxr-xr-x)                         │
         │                                                                  │
         │  umask = 027:                                                    │
         │    File: 666 - 027 = 640  (-rw-r-----)  ← group can read only   │
         │    Dir:  777 - 027 = 750  (drwxr-x---) ← other has NO access   │
         │                                                                  │
         │  umask = 077 (most restrictive):                                 │
         │    File: 666 - 077 = 600  (-rw-------)  ← owner-only           │
         │    Dir:  777 - 077 = 700  (drwx------)  ← owner-only           │
         │                                                                  │
         │  For anihpj service user: set umask 027 in the user's profile   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🛠️ A DAY IN THE LIFE OF A LINUX SYSADMIN — COMMAND WORKFLOW
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                   │
    │  MORNING ROUTINE — Check the server's health                      │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
    │  │  $ ssh admin@anihpj-prod                    (Ch 26 SSH)    │   │
