    │  │    09:15:23 up 45 days,  2 users, load avg: 0.52, 0.38, 0.41│   │
    │  │  $ df -hT                                    (Ch 23, 25)   │   │
    │  │    /dev/vg-data/lv-app ext4  48G  32G  14G  70% /var/www   │   │
    │  │  $ systemctl status anihpj nginx postgresql  (Ch 16)       │   │
    │  │  $ journalctl -u anihpj --since "1 hour ago" (Ch 17)       │   │
    │  │  $ tail -F /var/log/nginx/error.log          (Ch 5)        │   │
    │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
    │                              │                                     │
    │                              ▼                                     │
    │  MID-DAY — Deploy a new version of anihpj                         │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
    │  │  $ cd /var/www/anihpj                        (Ch 2)       │   │
    │  │  $ sudo -u anihpj git pull origin main       (Ch 12)      │   │

    │  │  $ tail -f /var/log/anihpj/app.log           (Ch 5)       │   │
    │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
    │                              │                                     │
    │                              ▼                                     │
    │  AFTERNOON — Troubleshoot an issue                                │
    │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
    │  │  "Users report 502 errors on /jobs/ page"                  │   │
    │  │  $ grep "502" /var/log/nginx/access.log     (Ch 6)        │   │

    │  │  $ journalctl -u anihpj -n 30                (Ch 17)       │   │
    │  │    ...gunicorn: connection refused (socket not found)      │   │
    │  │  $ ls -la /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock          (Ch 3, 4)      │   │
    │  │    srwxr-xr-x 1 anihpj www-data ... /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock│   │
    │  │  $ ss -tulnp | grep 8000                     (Ch 19)      │   │
    │  │    tcp LISTEN 0 128 0.0.0.0:8000 ... gunicorn (PID 12345) │   │
    │  │  $ sudo systemctl restart anihpj             (Ch 16)      │   │

Chapter 5: File & Directory Operations  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    5.1  Creating, Copying, Moving, Deleting
         - touch file.txt       → create empty file / update timestamp
         - mkdir dir            → create directory
         - mkdir -p a/b/c       → create nested directories at once
         - cp src dst           → copy file
         - cp -r srcdir dstdir  → copy directory recursively
         - mv src dst           → move or rename
         - rm file              → delete file
         - rm -rf dir           → delete directory and all contents ⚠️ IRREVERSIBLE
         [WARNING BOX: rm -rf has no undo — always double-check the path]

         [COMMON FILE OPERATIONS ON ANIHPJ]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Deploy new version of anihpj                                  │
         │  cp -r /tmp/anihpj-v2/ /var/www/anihpj/                         │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Backup config before editing                                  │
         │  cp /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj{,.bak}                    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Rename old log file                                           │
         │  mv /var/log/anihpj/app.log /var/log/anihpj/app.log.old         │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Remove compiled Python cache                                  │
         │  find /var/www/anihpj -name "*.pyc" -delete                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.2  Listing and Inspecting Files
         - ls -la        → long format, all files including hidden
         - ls -lh        → human-readable sizes (KB, MB, GB)
         - ls -lt        → sorted by modification time (newest first)
         - ls -lR        → recursive listing
         - stat file     → detailed metadata: size, inode, timestamps, permissions
         - file filename → determine file type regardless of extension
         [TABLE: ls options and what they show]

         [stat COMMAND OUTPUT — what every field means]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  $ stat /var/www/anihpj/manage.py                               │
         │    File: /var/www/anihpj/manage.py                              │
         │    Size: 686         Blocks: 8    IO Block: 4096  regular file  │
         │  Device: fd01h/64769d  Inode: 524308  Links: 1                  │
         │  Access: (0644/-rw-r--r--)  Uid: (1001/ anihpj) Gid:(33/www-data)│
         │  Access: 2026-05-24 18:00:01 ← last READ time (atime)           │
         │  Modify: 2026-05-20 14:30:45 ← last CONTENT change (mtime)      │
         │  Change: 2026-05-20 14:30:45 ← last METADATA change (ctime)    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  KEY: mtime = data changed | ctime = permissions/owner changed  │
         │  Exams use: find -mtime (content) vs find -ctime (metadata)     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ls OPTIONS — COMPLETE REFERENCE CARD]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  FLAG  │ MEANING                    │ EXAMPLE OUTPUT DIFFERENCE  │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  -l    │ Long format (detail view)  │ shows perms, owner, size   │
         │  -a    │ All (including hidden .)   │ shows .env, .bashrc etc    │
         │  -h    │ Human-readable sizes       │ 4.0K instead of 4096       │
         │  -t    │ Sort by modification time  │ newest files at top        │
         │  -r    │ Reverse sort order         │ oldest first (with -t)     │
         │  -R    │ Recursive (all subdirs)    │ shows /var/www/ tree       │
         │  -S    │ Sort by file SIZE          │ largest files first        │
         │  -i    │ Show inode number          │ 524308 manage.py           │
         │  -1    │ One file per line          │ useful for piping to wc -l │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.3  Finding Files — find, locate, which, whereis  🎯
         - find /path -name "*.py"         → search by name
         - find /var/www -user anihpj      → files owned by user
         - find / -perm /4000             → find all SUID files
         - find /var/log -mtime -1         → modified in last 24 hours
         - find /tmp -size +100M           → larger than 100MB
         - locate filename                 → fast search using database (updatedb)
         - which python3                   → where is this command?
         - whereis nginx                   → binary + man page + source locations
         [CHEAT CARD: find command — 20 most useful examples]

         [THE find COMMAND — THE LFCS POWER TOOL]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  WHAT YOU WANT               │ find COMMAND                     │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  All Python files in project │ find /var/www/anihpj -name "*.py"│
         │  Files modified today        │ find /var/log -mtime 0           │
         │  Dirs only (not files)       │ find /etc -type d                │
         │  Files only (not dirs)       │ find /var/www -type f            │
         │  Files owned by nginx        │ find /var/www -user nginx        │
         │  Files > 10MB                │ find / -size +10M                │
         │  Empty files                 │ find /tmp -empty                 │
         │  SUID binaries (security!)   │ find / -perm -4000 -type f       │
         │  Execute on each result      │ find . -name "*.log" -exec rm {} \; │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.4  Links — Hard Links and Symbolic Links  🎯
         - Hard link: another name for the SAME data on disk (same inode)
         - Symbolic link: a shortcut that POINTS to another file (different inode)
         - ln target link_name          → create hard link
         - ln -s target link_name       → create symbolic link (symlink)
         - Symlinks can span filesystems; hard links cannot
         - Deleting original: hard link still works; symlink becomes BROKEN
         - /usr/bin/python → /usr/bin/python3.11  (classic system symlink)
         - Nginx config: /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/ → ../sites-available/ (symlinks)
         [DIAGRAM: inode ← hard link A, hard link B | symlink → original file]

         [HARD LINK vs SYMBOLIC LINK — ILLUSTRATED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  HARD LINK:                                                      │
         │  manage.py  ──┐                                                  │
         │               ├──▶  inode 524308  ──▶  DISK DATA               │
         │  manage_link  ──┘                                                │
         │  (both filenames point to the SAME disk data)                   │
         │  → Delete one: other still works. Data survives.                │
         │  → Cannot cross filesystems. Cannot link to directories.        │
         │                                                                  │
         │  SYMBOLIC LINK:                                                  │
         │  python3  ──▶  inode 99001  ──▶  "points to /usr/bin/python3.11"│
         │                                         │                        │
         │                                         ▼                        │
         │                               inode 88400  ──▶  DISK DATA       │
         │  (symlink stores a PATH, not data)                               │
         │  → Delete target: symlink becomes 🔴 BROKEN (dangling link)     │
         │  → Can cross filesystems. Can link to directories.              │
         │                                                                  │
         │  REAL EXAMPLE — Nginx config with symlinks:                      │
         │  /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj  ← the REAL config file     │
         │  /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/anihpj    → symlink to above          │
         │    ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj \                    │
         │           /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/anihpj                       │
         │  (disable site: just remove the symlink, keep the real file)    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HARD LINK vs SYMLINK — COMPARISON TABLE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PROPERTY            │ HARD LINK         │ SYMBOLIC LINK        │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  Same inode?         │ YES ✅             │ NO — own inode       │
         │  Cross filesystem?   │ NO ❌              │ YES ✅               │
         │  Link to directory?  │ NO ❌              │ YES ✅               │
         │  Original deleted?   │ Data survives ✅   │ Link breaks ❌       │
         │  ls -la shows        │ -rw-r--r-- 2 ...  │ lrwxrwxrwx ... -> f  │
         │  Command             │ ln src link        │ ln -s src link       │
         │  LFCS use case       │ Rarely needed      │ Nginx, Python paths  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.5  Viewing & Reading Files  🎯
         - cat file           → print entire file to terminal (small files)
         - less file          → scrollable viewer (q=quit, /=search, n=next match)
         - more file          → older pager (less preferred; less is more!)
         - head -n 20 file    → first 20 lines (default: 10 lines)
         - tail -n 50 file    → last 50 lines (default: 10 lines)
         - tail -f file       → FOLLOW file in real-time (essential for logs!)
         - tail -F file       → follow + reopen if log rotated (better for logs)
         - wc -l file         → count lines
         - wc -w file         → count words
         - wc -c file         → count bytes
         - strings file       → extract readable text from binary files
         [SCENARIO: Monitor anihpj Django logs in real-time with tail -f]

         [FILE VIEWING TOOLS — WHEN TO USE WHICH]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TOOL       │ BEST FOR                │ KEY CONTROL              │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  cat        │ Small files, piping     │ cat file | grep pattern  │
         │  less       │ Large files, searching  │ /term  n  q  G  g        │
         │  head       │ First N lines of any    │ head -50 file            │
         │  tail -f    │ LIVE log monitoring     │ Ctrl+C to stop           │
         │  tail -F    │ Log-rotated files       │ Follows rotated logs     │
         │  strings    │ Binary file inspection  │ strings /bin/ls          │
         │  od / xxd   │ Hex dump of binary      │ xxd file | head          │
         │                                                                  │
         │  LIVE LOG MONITORING — anihpj in production:                    │
         │  tail -F /var/log/nginx/access.log &     ← background           │
         │  tail -F /var/log/anihpj/gunicorn.log &  ← background           │
         │  journalctl -fu anihpj.service           ← systemd logs live    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INSIDE less — NAVIGATION CHEAT CARD]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  q          → quit                                               │
         │  /pattern   → search forward (type pattern, Enter)              │
         │  n          → next search match                                  │
         │  N          → previous search match                              │
         │  G          → go to END of file                                  │
         │  g          → go to BEGINNING of file                            │
         │  Space      → next page                                          │
         │  b          → previous page                                      │
         │  :n         → next file (when multiple files open)              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.6  I/O Redirection and Pipes  🎯
         - >  file    → redirect stdout (overwrite)
         - >> file    → redirect stdout (append)
         - 2> file    → redirect stderr
         - 2>&1       → redirect stderr to stdout
         - < file     → redirect file as stdin
         - |          → pipe: send output of one command to next
         [CHAIN DIAGRAM: command1 | command2 | command3 — data flowing through]

         [REDIRECTION IN PRACTICE — anihpj deployment]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Save all pip install output to a log file                    │
         │  pip install -r requirements.txt > /var/log/anihpj/install.log │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Save both stdout AND stderr                                   │
         │  python manage.py migrate >> /var/log/anihpj/migrate.log 2>&1  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Count how many running anihpj processes exist                │
         │  ps aux | grep gunicorn | wc -l                                 │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Find which port nginx is listening on                        │
         │  ss -tulnp | grep nginx                                         │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Discard error messages (send to /dev/null)                   │
         │  find / -name "*.pyc" 2>/dev/null                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 6: Text Processing — Searching, Filtering, Transforming  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    6.1  grep — Searching Inside Files  🎯
         - grep "pattern" file        → find lines matching pattern
         - grep -r "ERROR" /var/log/  → recursive search
         - grep -i "django"           → case-insensitive
         - grep -n "pattern" file     → show line numbers
         - grep -v "DEBUG"            → invert: show lines NOT matching
         - grep -c "ERROR" file       → count matching lines only
         - grep -l "pattern" *.log    → show filenames only
         - grep -A 3 -B 2 "ERROR"     → show 3 lines After, 2 Before match
         [SCENARIO: Debugging anihpj — find all ERROR lines in Django logs]

    6.2  Regular Expressions — The Power of Pattern Matching  🎯
         - .         → any single character (except newline)
         - *         → zero or more of the preceding element
         - +         → one or more of preceding (grep -E or egrep)
         - ?         → zero or one of preceding (optional character)
         - ^         → start of line (anchor)
         - $         → end of line (anchor)
         - [abc]     → any single character: a OR b OR c
         - [^abc]    → any character EXCEPT a, b, c
         - [a-z]     → any lowercase letter (range)
         - {3}       → exactly 3 of preceding (grep -E)
         - {2,5}     → between 2 and 5 of preceding
         - \b        → word boundary (grep -E)
         - |         → OR (grep -E): cat|dog matches "cat" or "dog"
         - ()        → group: (ab)+ matches "ab", "abab", etc.
         [REGEX CHEAT CARD with 15 practical examples on /var/log/syslog]

         [REGEX VISUAL CHEAT CARD — with anihpj examples]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PATTERN         │ MATCHES                │ USE CASE            │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  ^ERROR          │ Lines starting w ERROR │ grep '^ERROR' app.log│
         │  ERROR$          │ Lines ending w ERROR   │ (less common)       │
         │  ERR.*           │ ERR + anything after   │ grep 'ERR.*' app.log │
         │  [0-9]{3}        │ Exactly 3 digits       │ HTTP status codes   │
         │  [0-9]\{1,3\}    │ 1 to 3 digits (BRE)   │ IP address segment  │
         │  ^$              │ Empty lines            │ sed '/^$/d' file    │
         │  ^#              │ Comment lines          │ grep -v '^#' nginx  │
         │  [A-Z][a-z]*     │ Capitalized word       │ Proper nouns        │
         │                                                                  │
         │  IP ADDRESS PATTERN (extended regex):                           │
         │  grep -E '[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}'     │
         │  (extract IPs from nginx access log)                            │
         │                                                                  │
         │  BASIC (BRE) vs EXTENDED (ERE) vs PERL (PRE):                  │
         │  grep 'pattern'     → BRE (basic, \ needed for +?{})           │
         │  grep -E 'pattern'  → ERE (extended, no escaping needed)       │
         │  grep -P 'pattern'  → PRE (Perl, supports \d \w \s lookahead)  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.3  sed — Stream Editor  🎯
         - sed 's/old/new/' file          → replace first match per line
         - sed 's/old/new/g' file         → replace ALL matches per line
         - sed -i 's/DEBUG/INFO/g' file   → edit file IN PLACE
         - sed -n '5,10p' file            → print only lines 5-10
         - sed '/^#/d' file               → delete comment lines
         - sed 's/localhost/anihpj.com/g' → update domain in config
         [SCENARIO: Bulk-update Django settings.py for production deployment]

    6.4  awk — Column-Based Data Processing  🎯
         - awk '{print $1}' file             → print first column (space-delimited)
         - awk -F: '{print $1}' /etc/passwd  → use : as field separator (usernames)
         - awk '$3 > 1000' /etc/passwd       → filter: only users with UID > 1000
         - awk '{sum += $1} END {print sum}' → sum column 1 across all rows
         - awk '/ERROR/ {print $0}' app.log  → print lines matching pattern
         - awk '{print NR, $0}' file         → add line numbers (NR = Number of Record)
         - awk 'END {print NR}' file         → count total lines (= wc -l)
         - awk '{print $NF}' file            → last column (NF = Number of Fields)
         [TABLE: awk vs grep vs sed — when to use which]

         [awk vs grep vs sed — WHEN TO USE WHICH]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TOOL  │ BEST FOR                          │ EXAMPLE             │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  grep  │ Finding matching lines            │ grep "ERROR" app.log│
         │  sed   │ Substituting/transforming text    │ sed 's/old/new/g'   │
         │  awk   │ Processing columns & arithmetic   │ awk '{print $3}'    │
         │                                                                  │
         │  DECISION TREE:                                                  │
         │  "I want to FIND lines"          → grep                         │
         │  "I want to REPLACE text"        → sed                          │
         │  "I want to EXTRACT columns"     → awk or cut                   │
         │  "I want to DO MATH on data"     → awk                          │
         │  "I want ALL THREE"              → pipe them: grep|sed|awk      │
         │                                                                  │
         │  ANIHPJ LOG ANALYSIS PIPELINE:                                   │
         │  cat /var/log/nginx/access.log                                   │
         │    | grep " 500 "                    ← filter 500 errors only   │
         │    | awk '{print $1}'                ← extract IP column        │
         │    | sort                            ← sort IPs                 │
         │    | uniq -c                         ← count per IP             │
         │    | sort -rn                        ← highest count first      │
         │    | head -10                        ← top 10 offenders         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.5  cut, sort, uniq, tr — The Data Pipeline Toolkit
         - cut -d: -f1 /etc/passwd        → first field, colon-delimited (usernames)
         - cut -d: -f1,3 /etc/passwd      → fields 1 AND 3 (username + UID)
         - sort file                      → alphabetical sort (A-Z)
         - sort -n file                   → numeric sort (1, 2, 10 not 1, 10, 2)
         - sort -rn file                  → reverse numeric (largest first)
         - sort -k2 -n file               → sort by column 2 numerically
         - sort -u file                   → sort + remove duplicates (= sort | uniq)
         - uniq file                      → remove ADJACENT duplicates (sort first!)
         - uniq -c file                   → prefix each line with occurrence count
         - uniq -d file                   → print only DUPLICATE lines
         - tr 'a-z' 'A-Z'                → translate lowercase to uppercase
         - tr -d '\r'                     → remove Windows carriage returns (CRLF fix)
         - tr -s ' '                      → squeeze repeated spaces into one
         [PIPELINE: cat log | grep ERROR | awk '{print $4}' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn]

         [THE POWER PIPELINE — log analysis for anihpj]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Find the most common HTTP errors in nginx access log          │
         │                                                                  │
         │  cat /var/log/nginx/access.log                                   │
         │    │                                                             │
         │    ├──▶ grep " [45][0-9][0-9] "   (filter 4xx and 5xx errors)  │
         │    │                                                             │
         │    ├──▶ awk '{print $9}'           (extract the status code)    │
         │    │                                                             │
         │    ├──▶ sort                       (sort all status codes)      │
         │    │                                                             │
         │    ├──▶ uniq -c                    (count each unique code)     │
         │    │                                                             │
         │    └──▶ sort -rn                   (highest count first)        │
         │                                                                  │
         │  OUTPUT:                                                         │
         │     143 500                        (500 Internal Server Error)   │
         │      87 404                        (404 Not Found)               │
         │      12 403                        (403 Forbidden)               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.6  diff, patch, comm — Comparing Files
         - diff file1 file2             → show differences between two files
         - diff -u file1 file2          → unified diff (standard patch format)
         - diff -r dir1/ dir2/          → compare directory trees
         - patch file < patchfile       → apply a diff as a patch
         [SCENARIO: diff old nginx.conf new.conf before applying changes]


Chapter 7: File Archives & Compression  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    7.1  tar — The Archiving Standard  🎯
         - tar -cvf archive.tar dir/     → create archive (verbose)
         - tar -xvf archive.tar          → extract archive
         - tar -czvf archive.tar.gz dir/ → create compressed with gzip
         - tar -cjvf archive.tar.bz2 dir/→ create compressed with bzip2
         - tar -cJvf archive.tar.xz dir/ → create compressed with xz
         - tar -tvf archive.tar          → list contents without extracting
         - tar -xzvf archive.tar.gz -C /opt/ → extract to specific directory
         [MEMORY AID: "c=Create, x=eXtract, v=Verbose, f=File, z=gzip, j=bzip2"]

         [TAR COMMAND CHEAT CARD]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TASK                │ COMMAND                                   │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  Backup anihpj dir   │ tar -czvf anihpj-$(date +%F).tar.gz \   │
         │                      │   /var/www/anihpj/                       │
         │  List archive        │ tar -tvf anihpj-2026-05-24.tar.gz        │
         │  Extract to /opt     │ tar -xzvf anihpj.tar.gz -C /opt/        │
         │  Extract one file    │ tar -xzvf anihpj.tar.gz ./manage.py     │
         │  Check integrity     │ gzip -t anihpj.tar.gz                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.2  gzip, bzip2, xz — Compression Tools
         - gzip file           → compress (creates file.gz, removes original)
         - gzip -d file.gz     → decompress (= gunzip file.gz)
         - gzip -k file        → compress and KEEP the original file
         - gzip -9 file        → maximum compression (slower)
         - bzip2 / bunzip2     → better compression ratio, slower than gzip
         - xz / unxz           → best compression ratio, slowest (ideal for backups)
         - zip / unzip         → Windows-compatible, for cross-platform sharing
         - zcat / bzcat / xzcat → read compressed file WITHOUT decompressing
         [COMPARISON TABLE: gzip vs bzip2 vs xz — speed vs compression ratio]

         [COMPRESSION TOOLS — SPEED vs SIZE TRADEOFF]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  SPEED          ◀──────────────────────────────▶  SIZE (smaller)│
         │  (faster)                                           (better)    │
         │                                                                  │
         │   gzip     ████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  Fast, medium       │
         │   bzip2    ███████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  Medium, good       │
         │   xz       █████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  Slow, BEST ratio  │
         │   zip      ████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  Fast, Windows too  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  COMPARISON ON anihpj-backup/ (100 MB uncompressed):            │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  TOOL   │ COMMAND            │ OUTPUT SIZE  │ TIME        │  │
         │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │  │
         │  │  gzip   │ tar -czvf x.tar.gz │ ~35 MB       │ 5 sec      │  │
         │  │  bzip2  │ tar -cjvf x.tar.bz2│ ~30 MB       │ 25 sec     │  │
         │  │  xz     │ tar -cJvf x.tar.xz │ ~25 MB       │ 90 sec     │  │
         │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                                  │
         │  RECOMMENDATION FOR anihpj BACKUPS:                             │
         │    Daily quick backup   → gzip (fast, good enough)             │
         │    Weekly archive       → xz (smaller, store long-term)        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.3  Backup Strategies for anihpj  🎯
         - Full backup: tar the entire project directory
         - Database backup: pg_dump for PostgreSQL
         - Incremental with rsync: only copy CHANGED files (bandwidth-efficient)
         - rsync -avz /var/www/anihpj/ user@backup-server:/backups/anihpj/
         - rsync --delete: remove files on destination if deleted from source
         - Automate with cron: run backups daily at 2 AM without intervention
         [WORKFLOW DIAGRAM: Backup pipeline with cron scheduling]

         [ANIHPJ COMPLETE BACKUP STRATEGY DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  WHAT TO BACKUP          │  HOW                  │  WHEN        │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  /var/www/anihpj/        │  tar -cJvf            │  Daily 2AM   │
         │  (source code + static)  │                        │              │
         │                          │                        │              │
         │  PostgreSQL database     │  pg_dump anihpj_db \  │  Daily 1AM   │
         │  (jobpost models data)   │  > anihpj_$(date+%F).sql│            │
         │                          │                        │              │
         │  /etc/nginx/             │  tar -czvf nginx.tar.gz│  Weekly      │
         │  (nginx config)          │                        │              │
         │                          │                        │              │
         │  /etc/systemd/system/    │  tar -czvf svc.tar.gz  │  On change   │
         │  (service files)         │                        │              │
         │                                                                  │
         │  BACKUP PIPELINE (automated script + cron):                     │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  #!/bin/bash                                              │   │
         │  │  DATE=$(date +%Y-%m-%d)                                   │   │
         │  │  BACKUP_DIR=/backups/anihpj/$DATE                        │   │
         │  │  mkdir -p $BACKUP_DIR                                     │   │
         │  │                                                            │   │
         │  │  # Code backup                                             │   │
         │  │  tar -cJf $BACKUP_DIR/code.tar.xz /var/www/anihpj/       │   │
         │  │                                                            │   │
         │  │  # DB backup                                               │   │
         │  │  pg_dump anihpj_db > $BACKUP_DIR/db.sql                   │   │
         │  │                                                            │   │
         │  │  # Send offsite with rsync                                 │   │
         │  │  rsync -avz $BACKUP_DIR/ backup@remote:/backups/anihpj/  │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 8: Package Management — Installing Software  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    8.1  APT — Advanced Package Tool (Ubuntu/Debian)  🎯
         - apt update               → update package index (list of available versions)
         - apt upgrade              → install available upgrades
         - apt install nginx        → install a package
         - apt remove nginx         → uninstall (keep config files)
         - apt purge nginx          → uninstall + remove config files
         - apt search "web server"  → search available packages
         - apt show nginx           → package details and dependencies
         - apt list --installed     → all installed packages
         [SCENARIO: Full anihpj stack installation with apt]

         [APT WORKFLOW FOR ANIHPJ SERVER SETUP]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Update package lists first — ALWAYS do this first            │
         │  apt update                                                      │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Install required packages for anihpj                         │
         │  apt install -y nginx postgresql postgresql-client \             │
         │    python3 python3-pip python3-venv git                          │
         │                                                                  │
         │  # Check installed version                                       │
         │  nginx -v                                                        │
         │  psql --version                                                  │
         │  python3 --version                                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.2  DNF / YUM — Package Manager (Rocky Linux / RHEL)  🎯
         - dnf update               → update all packages
         - dnf install nginx        → install package
         - dnf remove nginx         → remove package
         - dnf search nginx         → search packages by name/description
         - dnf info nginx           → package details (version, size, repo)
         - dnf list installed       → all installed packages
         - dnf provides /usr/bin/python3 → what package provides this file?
         - dnf history              → show history of all dnf operations
         - dnf group install "Development Tools" → install a package group
         [SIDE-BY-SIDE TABLE: apt vs dnf equivalent commands]

         [APT vs DNF — EQUIVALENT COMMANDS SIDE BY SIDE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TASK                    │ APT (Ubuntu)           │ DNF (Rocky) │
         │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  Update package index    │ apt update             │ dnf check-update│
         │  Upgrade all packages    │ apt upgrade            │ dnf update     │
         │  Install a package       │ apt install nginx      │ dnf install nginx│
         │  Remove a package        │ apt remove nginx       │ dnf remove nginx │
         │  Remove + config files   │ apt purge nginx        │ dnf remove nginx │
         │  Search packages         │ apt search nginx       │ dnf search nginx │
         │  Package info            │ apt show nginx         │ dnf info nginx   │
         │  List installed          │ apt list --installed   │ dnf list installed│
         │  What provides file?     │ dpkg -S /path/file     │ dnf provides /path│
         │  List files in package   │ dpkg -L packagename    │ rpm -ql packagename│
         │  Install local .deb/.rpm │ dpkg -i file.deb       │ dnf install ./f.rpm│
         │  Package history         │ /var/log/apt/history   │ dnf history       │
         │                                                                  │
         │  💡 LFCS TESTS BOTH — practice on Ubuntu AND Rocky Linux VM     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.3  Managing Package Repositories  🎯
         - /etc/apt/sources.list        → Ubuntu: main repo list
         - /etc/apt/sources.list.d/     → Ubuntu: additional repos (one file each)
         - add-apt-repository ppa:...   → add a PPA (Personal Package Archive)
         - apt-key add / gpg            → trust the signing key of a new repo
         - /etc/yum.repos.d/            → DNF/YUM: one .repo file per repository
         - dnf config-manager --add-repo=URL → add a repo on Rocky Linux
         [SCENARIO: Add Nginx mainline repo for latest version]

         [APT REPOSITORY STRUCTURE DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  /etc/apt/sources.list                                           │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ deb http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu jammy main restricted│   │
         │  │ deb http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu jammy-updates main  │   │
         │  │ deb http://security.ubuntu.com/ubuntu jammy-security main│   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                                  │
         │  /etc/apt/sources.list.d/   ← additional repos go here          │
         │  ├── nginx.list             ← Nginx official repo               │
         │  ├── postgresql.list        ← PostgreSQL official repo          │
         │  └── nodesource.list        ← Node.js official repo             │
         │                                                                  │
         │  ADD NGINX MAINLINE (example):                                   │
         │  echo "deb http://nginx.org/packages/ubuntu/ jammy nginx" \     │
         │    > /etc/apt/sources.list.d/nginx.list                         │
         │  curl -fsSL https://nginx.org/keys/nginx_signing.key | \        │
         │    gpg --dearmor > /etc/apt/trusted.gpg.d/nginx.gpg             │
         │  apt update && apt install nginx                                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.4  Compiling from Source (when no package exists)
         - When: software too new for repos, custom compile flags needed
         - Step 1: apt install build-essential (Ubuntu) / dnf group install "Dev Tools"
         - Step 2: Download tarball: wget https://example.com/app-1.0.tar.gz
         - Step 3: Extract: tar -xzvf app-1.0.tar.gz && cd app-1.0
         - Step 4: Configure: ./configure --prefix=/usr/local
         - Step 5: Compile: make -j$(nproc)  (uses all CPU cores)
         - Step 6: Install: make install
         - Uninstall: make uninstall (if supported) — or track files manually
         [WARNING BOX: Package manager STRONGLY preferred — source install = manual updates forever]

         [COMPILE FROM SOURCE — STEP BY STEP FLOW]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  ① PREREQUISITES                                                  │
         │    apt install build-essential gcc make libssl-dev               │
         │              │                                                   │
         │              ▼                                                   │
         │  ② DOWNLOAD & EXTRACT                                            │
         │    wget https://site.com/app-1.0.tar.gz                         │
         │    tar -xzvf app-1.0.tar.gz && cd app-1.0/                      │
         │              │                                                   │
         │              ▼                                                   │
         │  ③ CONFIGURE (check dependencies, set install path)              │
         │    ./configure --prefix=/usr/local                               │
         │    (reads: configure.ac, Makefile.am → generates Makefile)      │
         │              │                                                   │
         │              ▼                                                   │
         │  ④ COMPILE (takes time, uses CPU)                                │
         │    make -j$(nproc)   ← parallel jobs = faster                   │
         │              │                                                   │
         │              ▼                                                   │
         │  ⑤ INSTALL (copies binaries to --prefix path)                    │
         │    sudo make install                                             │
         │    which app  →  /usr/local/bin/app  ✅                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 3: TEXT PROCESSING & SHELL SCRIPTING — AUTOMATION FUNDAMENTALS      🟢🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    🔗 THE UNIX PIPELINE PHILOSOPHY — "DO ONE THING WELL"
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                   │
    │  Unix tools are like LEGO bricks — each does ONE thing, but      │
    │  you can SNAP them together with pipes (|) to build complex       │
    │  data processing pipelines.                                       │
    │                                                                   │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │  THE ANIHPJ LOG ANALYSIS PIPELINE                            │ │
    │  │                                                               │ │
    │  │  $ cat /var/log/nginx/access.log                              │ │
    │  │    │  (read the raw log file — 500,000+ lines)                │ │
    │  │    │                                                           │ │
    │  │    ├── grep " 500 "                                           │ │
    │  │    │   (keep only lines with HTTP 500 errors)                 │ │
    │  │    │   📊 500,000 → 143 lines                                  │ │
    │  │    │                                                           │ │
    │  │    ├── awk '{print $1}'                                       │ │
    │  │    │   (extract just the IP address — column 1)              │ │
    │  │    │   📊 143 IP addresses                                     │ │
    │  │    │                                                           │ │
    │  │    ├── sort                                                    │ │
    │  │    │   (sort alphabetically so uniq can work)                 │ │
    │  │    │   📊 203.0.113.5, 203.0.113.5, 198.51.100.2...         │ │
    │  │    │                                                           │ │
    │  │    ├── uniq -c                                                │ │
    │  │    │   (count how many times each IP appears)                 │ │
    │  │    │   📊 45 203.0.113.5, 12 198.51.100.2...                 │ │
    │  │    │                                                           │ │
    │  │    ├── sort -rn                                               │ │
    │  │    │   (sort by count, largest first)                         │ │
    │  │    │   📊 most problematic IPs at top                          │ │
    │  │    │                                                           │ │
    │  │    └── head -10                                               │ │
    │  │        (show only the top 10 offenders)                       │ │
    │  │        📊 final answer!                                        │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                                                                   │
    │  🎯 ONE-LINER VERSION:                                            │
    │  cat access.log | grep " 500 " | awk '{print $1}' | sort \      │
    │    | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10                                │
    │                                                                   │
    │  This is the Unix philosophy in action: each tool (cat, grep,    │
    │  awk, sort, uniq, head) does one thing perfectly. Connected      │
    │  with pipes, they answer complex questions in seconds.           │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    📜 BASH SCRIPTING DECISION TREE — WHEN TO WRITE A SCRIPT
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                   │
    │  "Should I write a bash script for this?"                        │
    │           │                                                       │
    │           ▼                                                       │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────┐                                      │
    │  │ Will you run this       │                                      │
    │  │ command more than ONCE? │                                      │
    │  └──────────┬──────────────┘                                      │
    │             │                                                      │
    │        ┌────┴────┐                                                 │
    │        NO        YES                                               │
    │        │         │                                                 │
    │        ▼         ▼                                                 │
    │    ┌────────┐ ┌─────────────────────────┐                         │
    │    │ Just   │ │ Is it more than 5 lines │                         │
    │    │ run it │ │ of commands?            │                         │
    │    │ once   │ └──────────┬──────────────┘                         │
    │    └────────┘            │                                         │
    │                     ┌────┴────┐                                     │
    │                     NO        YES                                   │
    │                     │         │                                     │
    │                     ▼         ▼                                     │
    │                 ┌────────┐ ┌─────────────────────────┐            │
    │                 │ Create │ │ Will others on the team │            │
    │                 │ alias  │ │ need to run it too?     │            │
    │                 └────────┘ └──────────┬──────────────┘            │
    │                                  ┌────┴────┐                       │
    │                                  NO        YES                     │
    │                                  │         │                       │
    │                                  ▼         ▼                       │
    │                              ┌────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐     │
    │                              │ Simple │ │ WRITE A BASH      │     │
    │                              │ script │ │ SCRIPT with:      │     │
    │                              │ is fine│ │ • --help message  │     │
    │                              └────────┘ │ • Error handling  │     │
    │                                         │ • Logging         │     │
    │                                         │ • set -euo pipefail│     │
    │                                         └───────────────────┘     │
    │                                                                   │
    │  ANIHPJ SCRIPTS WORTH WRITING:                                    │
    │  ✅ deploy.sh      → multi-step, run daily, team uses it          │
    │  ✅ backup.sh      → complex pipeline, cron runs it               │
    │  ✅ health-check.sh → used by monitoring, needs error codes       │
    │  ❌ ls -la          → just an alias, no script needed              │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 9: Advanced Text Processing & Regular Expressions
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    9.1  Combining Tools — The Unix Philosophy
         - "Do one thing well; work together; use text streams"
         - Pipelines: grep | awk | sort | uniq — building data flows
         - xargs: pass pipe output as arguments to commands
         [PIPELINE DIAGRAM: log file → grep → awk → sort → uniq -c → analysis]

    9.2  grep Advanced — Extended Regex, Perl Regex
         - grep -E or egrep — Extended regex (supports +, ?, |, {})
         - grep -P         — Perl-compatible regex (most powerful)
         - grep -o         — print ONLY the matching part (not whole line)
         - grep -m 5       — stop after 5 matches
         [EXAMPLES: Extract all IP addresses from nginx access.log]

    9.3  sed Advanced — Multiple Expressions, Addresses, Scripts
         - sed -e 's/a/b/' -e 's/c/d/' → multiple substitutions
         - sed '1d' file     → delete line 1
         - sed '$d' file     → delete last line
         - sed '2,5d' file   → delete lines 2 through 5
         - sed '/pattern/d'  → delete lines matching pattern
         - sed -f script.sed → run sed commands from file
         [PRACTICAL: Clean up anihpj error logs, remove DEBUG entries]

         [grep vs sed vs awk — WHEN TO USE WHICH (DECISION TREE)]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                  │
         │  "I need to process text from a file/pipe..."                   │
         │           │                                                       │
         │           ▼                                                       │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐                     │
         │  │ What do you want to DO with the text?   │                     │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘                     │
         │           │                                                       │
         │     ┌─────┼─────────────┬──────────────────┐                     │
         │     ▼     ▼             ▼                  ▼                     │
         │  FIND lines  REPLACE text  EXTRACT columns  DO MATH              │
         │     │          │             │                │                   │
         │     ▼          ▼             ▼                ▼                   │
         │  ┌──────┐  ┌──────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐            │
         │  │ grep │  │ sed  │    │cut -d: -f1│    │awk '{sum │            │
         │  │      │  │      │    │  OR       │    │ += $1}   │            │
         │  │-i case│  │s/old/│    │awk '{print│    │END {print│            │
         │  │-v invert│ │new/g│    │  $1}'     │    │  sum}'   │            │
         │  │-E regex│ │-i inline│ │           │    │          │            │
         │  │-c count│ │/^#/d   │ │           │    │          │            │
         │  └──────┘  └──────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘            │
         │                                                                  │
         │  ANIHPJ LOG ANALYSIS EXAMPLES:                                   │
         │  grep "ERROR" app.log              → FIND error lines           │
         │  sed 's/localhost/anihpj.com/g'     → REPLACE domain             │
         │  awk '{print $1, $9}' access.log    → EXTRACT IP + status code  │
         │  awk '{sum+=$10} END {print sum}'   → SUM bytes transferred     │
         │                                                                  │
         │  🔗 PIPELINE: grep " 500 " access.log | awk '{print $1}' \     │
         │              | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn                        │
         │  (FIND + EXTRACT + COUNT = all three working together!)         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.4  awk Advanced — Functions, Arrays, Multifile Processing
         - awk 'NR==FNR{a[$1]=$2;next} $1 in a {print $0,a[$1]}' f1 f2
         - awk 'BEGIN{} {processing} END{}' → lifecycle blocks
         - awk arrays for counting and grouping
         - printf formatting for clean output tables
         [REAL USE: Analyze nginx access.log — requests per IP per hour]

    9.5  Useful One-Liners for System Administration
         - Find top 10 largest files in /var: du -ah /var | sort -rh | head -10
         - Count 404 errors in nginx log: grep " 404 " access.log | wc -l
         - Show unique IPs hitting anihpj: awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort -u
         - Remove blank lines: sed '/^$/d' file
         - Add line numbers: cat -n file
         - Reverse file: tac file
         [COLLECTION BOX: 20 essential sysadmin one-liners]


Chapter 10: Bash Scripting — Automating Linux Administration
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    10.1  Your First Shell Script
          - Shebang line: #!/bin/bash (tells kernel which interpreter to use)
          - chmod +x script.sh to make executable
          - ./script.sh to run (or bash script.sh)
          - Exit codes: 0 = success, non-zero = error
          - $? → the exit code of the last command
          [FIRST SCRIPT: Check if anihpj service is running]

          [ANATOMY OF A BASH SCRIPT]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  #!/bin/bash                 ← shebang: use bash interpreter    │
          │  # Check anihpj service      ← comment (ignored by bash)        │
          │                                                                  │
          │  SERVICE="anihpj"                                                │
          │                                                                  │
          │  if systemctl is-active --quiet $SERVICE; then                  │
          │      echo "✅ $SERVICE is running"                               │
          │  else                                                            │
          │      echo "❌ $SERVICE is DOWN — restarting..."                  │
          │      systemctl start $SERVICE                                    │
          │  fi                                                              │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.2  Variables
          - VAR="value"         → assignment (no spaces around =)
          - echo $VAR           → use variable
          - echo "${VAR}text"   → variable in string context
          - readonly VAR        → make immutable
          - unset VAR           → remove variable
          - Environment vars: export VAR  (visible to child processes)
          - Special: $0=script name, $1-$9=arguments, $#=argument count, $@=all args

    10.3  User Input & Arguments
          - read -p "Enter name: " NAME    → prompt user for input
          - $1, $2 ... $9                  → positional arguments
          - $@                             → all arguments as array
          - shift                          → shift arguments left
          [SCRIPT: Deploy anihpj with version number as argument]

    10.4  Conditionals — if/elif/else
          - if [ condition ]; then ... elif ... else ... fi
          - Test operators: -f (file exists), -d (dir), -z (empty string),
            -n (non-empty), -eq, -ne, -lt, -gt, -le, -ge (numbers),
            == != (strings), -e (exists), -r -w -x (permissions)
          [COMPARISON TABLE: [ ] vs [[ ]] vs (()) — when to use each]

    10.5  Loops — for, while, until
          - for f in *.py; do ... done     → iterate over files
          - for i in {1..10}; do ... done  → numeric range
          - while [ condition ]; do ... done
          - while read line; do ... done < file.txt   → read file line by line
          [SCRIPT: Batch process all anihpj log files, rotate older than 7 days]

    10.6  Functions
          - function_name() { commands; }
          - Return values with echo + command substitution
          - Local variables: local var="value"
          - Source functions from library file: source ./functions.sh
          [LIBRARY: Create anihpj admin function library]

    10.7  Error Handling and Logging
          - set -e         → exit on any error
          - set -u         → error on undefined variable
          - set -x         → print each command before running (debug mode)
          - trap           → run cleanup on exit, interrupt, or error
          [TEMPLATE: Production-ready script with full error handling]

    11.1  Deployment Script
          - Pull new code from Git
          - Install/update Python dependencies

          - Exit 0 if healthy, exit 1 if any issue
