Chapter 26: SSH Server — Secure Remote Access  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    26.1  How SSH Works — The Protocol
          - SSH encrypts all data between client and server
          - Port 22 by default
          - Authentication methods: password, public key, certificate
          - Key exchange, session encryption, integrity checking
          [DIAGRAM: SSH handshake — client hello → server key → session key]

    26.2  Installing and Configuring sshd  🎯
          - apt install openssh-server
          - /etc/ssh/sshd_config → main daemon config
          - systemctl status sshd → check if running
          - sshd -t → test config syntax before reloading
          [COMPLETE sshd_config for anihpj production server]

    26.3  SSH Key-Based Authentication  🎯
          - ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "anihpj-deploy"  → generate key pair
          - ssh-copy-id user@server    → copy public key to server
          - ~/.ssh/authorized_keys     → server stores allowed public keys
          - ~/.ssh/config              → client config (aliases, options)
          - chmod 700 ~/.ssh / chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys → must be correct!
          [SECURITY BOX: SSH key is your identity — never share the private key]

    26.4  SSH Port Forwarding and Tunneling
          - Local forward:  ssh -L 8080:localhost:80 user@server
          - Remote forward: ssh -R 8080:localhost:80 user@server
          - Dynamic SOCKS:  ssh -D 1080 user@server
          [SCENARIO: Access anihpj PostgreSQL admin via SSH tunnel (not exposed publicly)]

    26.5  SSH Config File — ~/.ssh/config
          - Host aliases, default user, identity file, port
          [EXAMPLE: ssh anihpj → connects to full server address with key]


Chapter 27: Web Server — Nginx & Apache  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    27.1  Nginx vs Apache — Architecture Differences
          - Nginx: event-driven, async, handles C10K, better for static files
          - Apache: process/thread per connection, more modules, .htaccess support
          - For anihpj: Nginx as reverse proxy in front of Gunicorn
          [COMPARISON TABLE: Nginx vs Apache — performance, use cases, config style]

    27.2  Installing and Configuring Nginx  🎯
          - apt install nginx
          - /etc/nginx/nginx.conf          → main config
          - /etc/nginx/sites-available/    → virtual host configs
          - /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/      → symlinks to active configs
          - nginx -t                       → test config syntax
          - systemctl reload nginx         → apply config without downtime
          [CONFIGURATION STRUCTURE DIAGRAM]

    27.3  Nginx Virtual Host for anihpj  🎯
          - Server block: listen, server_name, root, access_log, error_log
          - Location blocks: static files served directly, / proxied to Gunicorn
          - proxy_pass, proxy_set_header, proxy_http_version
          - Client max body size (for file uploads in jobpost app)
          [COMPLETE NGINX CONFIG for anihpj with annotations for each directive]

          [NGINX CONFIG FOR ANIHPJ — reverse proxy to Gunicorn]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  server {                                                        │
          │      listen 80;                                                  │
          │      server_name anihpj.com www.anihpj.com;                    │
          │                                                                  │
          │      # Static and media files served directly by Nginx          │
          │      location /static/ {                                         │
          │          alias /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/;                   │
          │      }                                                           │
          │      location /media/ {                                          │
          │          alias /var/www/anihpj/media/;                         │
          │      }                                                           │
          │                                                                  │
          │      # Everything else goes to Gunicorn                         │
          │      location / {                                                │
          │          proxy_pass http://unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock;       │
          │          proxy_set_header Host $http_host;                      │
          │          proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;              │
          │          proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for; │
          │          client_max_body_size 10M;                              │
          │      }                                                           │
          │  }                                                               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    27.4  SSL/TLS with Let's Encrypt (Certbot)  🎯
          - apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginx
          - certbot --nginx -d anihpj.com -d www.anihpj.com
          - certbot renew --dry-run           → test auto-renewal
          - /etc/letsencrypt/live/anihpj.com/ → certificate files
          - crontab for renewal check (certbot auto-installs this)
          [SCENARIO: Enable HTTPS for anihpj job board — zero-downtime]

          [HOW HTTPS/TLS WORKS — THE HANDSHAKE (simplified for LFCS)]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                  │
          │  BROWSER (Client)                 NGINX (Server - anihpj.com)   │
          │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ① CLIENT HELLO ─────────────────────▶                          │
          │     "I support TLS 1.3, these cipher suites:                    │
          │      AES-256-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305..."                         │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ②                          ◀────────────── SERVER HELLO         │
          │                                "Let's use TLS 1.3 + AES-256-GCM" │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ③                          ◀────────────── SERVER CERTIFICATE   │
          │                                "Here's my cert, signed by Let's  │
          │                                 Encrypt (trusted CA). My public  │
          │                                 key is inside. Verify me."      │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ④ CERTIFICATE VERIFICATION                                     │
          │     • Is it signed by a trusted CA? ✅                           │
          │     • Is it for anihpj.com? ✅ (CN/SAN match)                   │
          │     • Has it expired? ✅ (valid until cert expiry)              │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ⑤ KEY EXCHANGE (Diffie-Hellman)                                │
          │     Both sides agree on a SHARED SECRET without sending it.     │
          │     (Even if someone snoops, they can't figure out the key.)    │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ⑥ SYMMETRIC ENCRYPTION BEGINS                                  │
          │     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
          │     │  🔒 ALL DATA NOW ENCRYPTED WITH AES-256-GCM         │     │
          │     │  GET /jobs/ HTTP/1.1  →  [ENCRYPTED] →  Nginx      │     │
          │     │  HTTP/1.1 200 OK      ←  [ENCRYPTED] ←  anihpj     │     │
          │     └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
          │                                                                  │
          │  🎯 LFCS RELEVANCE: You configure the SERVER side (steps 2-3).  │
          │     • Generate keys (openssl genrsa / ssh-keygen)               │
          │     • Create CSRs (openssl req -new)                             │
          │     • Install certs (certbot, or manually in /etc/ssl/)         │
          │     • Check expiry (openssl x509 -enddate)                      │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    27.5  Apache2 Basics (for LFCS exam coverage)  🎯
          - apt install apache2
          - /etc/apache2/apache2.conf    → main config
          - /etc/apache2/sites-available/→ virtual host configs
          - a2ensite, a2dissite         → enable/disable sites
          - a2enmod, a2dismod           → enable/disable modules
          - apache2ctl -t               → test config
          [VIRTUAL HOST CONFIG for comparison with Nginx]


Chapter 28: PostgreSQL — Database Server Configuration  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    28.1  Installing PostgreSQL  🎯
          - apt install postgresql postgresql-contrib
          - /etc/postgresql/14/main/    → config directory
          - /var/lib/postgresql/        → data directory
          - systemctl status postgresql → check if running
          - postgres user: default superuser account (switch with sudo -u postgres)

    28.2  PostgreSQL Configuration Files  🎯
          - postgresql.conf  → server settings (port, memory, connections)
          - pg_hba.conf      → client authentication (who can connect, how)
          - listen_addresses = 'localhost' → only local connections (default/secure)
          - max_connections  → how many simultaneous connections
          [ANNOTATED pg_hba.conf showing line format: type db user addr method]

    28.3  Creating the anihpj Database and User  🎯
          - sudo -u postgres psql        → connect as postgres superuser
          - CREATE DATABASE anihpj_db;
          - CREATE USER anihpj_user WITH PASSWORD 'strongpassword';
          - GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON DATABASE anihpj_db TO anihpj_user;
          - ALTER DATABASE anihpj_db OWNER TO anihpj_user;
          [SQL BLOCK with all commands needed to set up anihpj database]

    28.4  Database Backup and Restore  🎯
          - pg_dump anihpj_db > /backups/anihpj_$(date +%F).sql
          - pg_dump -Fc anihpj_db > /backups/anihpj.dump  → custom format
          - pg_restore -d anihpj_db /backups/anihpj.dump  → restore
          - pg_dumpall > all_databases.sql                 → backup everything
          [COMPLETE BACKUP SCRIPT with rotation and cron schedule]

    28.5  PostgreSQL Performance Basics
          - shared_buffers: 25% of RAM (e.g. 1GB on 4GB server)
          - effective_cache_size: 75% of RAM
          - work_mem: per-query sort memory
          - vacuum, analyze: maintaining table statistics
          [TABLE: Key postgresql.conf settings with recommended values for anihpj]


Chapter 29: Email, Time Services & FTP  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    29.1  Time Synchronization — NTP & Chrony  🎯
          - timedatectl                    → view timezone and sync status
          - timedatectl set-timezone Africa/Lagos  → set timezone
          - timedatectl set-ntp true       → enable NTP sync
          - apt install chrony             → install Chrony (recommended)
          - /etc/chrony.conf               → NTP server config
          - chronyc tracking               → sync status
          - chronyc sources -v             → NTP sources
          [IMPORTANCE BOX: Correct time = valid SSL certs, correct log timestamps]

    29.2  Postfix — Basic Mail Transfer Agent  🎯
          - apt install postfix mailutils
          - /etc/postfix/main.cf          → main config
          - myhostname, mydomain, inet_interfaces settings
          - Relay mode: send via Gmail/AWS SES for anihpj notifications
          - mail -s "Subject" user@example.com → send test email
          - Alias: /etc/aliases → redirect root mail to admin

    29.3  vsftpd — FTP Server Basics  🎯
          - apt install vsftpd
          - /etc/vsftpd.conf              → config file
          - anonymous_enable=NO           → no anonymous access
          - local_enable=YES              → allow local users
          - chroot_local_user=YES         → jail users to their home
          [SECURITY BOX: Use SFTP (SSH-based) instead of FTP — encrypted by default]


Chapter 30: Running anihpj in Production — The Complete Stack  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    30.1  The Full anihpj Production Stack
          - Nginx (reverse proxy + SSL termination + static files)
          - Gunicorn (WSGI application server, UNIX socket)
          - Django (the web application, managed by Gunicorn)
          - PostgreSQL (relational database on localhost)
          - Redis (optional: Celery task queue / caching)
          - All as systemd services, auto-start on boot
          [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: Full anihpj server stack with connections]

          [ANIHPJ PRODUCTION ARCHITECTURE]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                  │
          │  Internet                                                        │
          │      │ HTTPS :443                                                │
          │      ▼                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
          │  │  UFW Firewall: allow 80, 443 only                    │       │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
          │      │                                                            │
          │      ▼                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
          │  │  Nginx :80/:443 (SSL, static files, reverse proxy)   │       │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
          │      │ UNIX socket: /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock                     │
          │      ▼                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
          │  │  Gunicorn (3 workers, user=anihpj)                   │       │
          │  │    → Django app (anihpj/wsgi.py)                     │       │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
          │      │ localhost:5432                                             │
          │      ▼                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
          │  │  PostgreSQL (user=anihpj_user, db=anihpj_db)         │       │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
          │                                                                  │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.2  Step-by-Step Production Deployment Checklist
          1. Install Ubuntu 22.04 LTS on your server
          2. Update packages, configure hostname, timezone
          3. Create anihpj service user (no login shell)
          4. Install Python 3, create virtualenv, install dependencies
          5. Configure PostgreSQL: database, user, passwords
          6. Set environment variables in .env file (chmod 600)
          7. Run Django migrations and collectstatic
          8. Create systemd service unit for Gunicorn
          9. Install and configure Nginx as reverse proxy
          10. Get SSL certificate with certbot
          11. Configure UFW firewall (allow 22, 80, 443)
          12. Enable all services: systemctl enable anihpj nginx postgresql
          13. Test: curl, browser, check all logs
          [COMPLETE BASH DEPLOYMENT SCRIPT for automated setup]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 9: SECURITY & HARDENING — PROTECTING YOUR LINUX SERVER              🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    🛡️ DEFENSE IN DEPTH — THE ANIHPJ SECURITY ONION (7 LAYERS)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                   │
    │  Layer 7: APPLICATION SECURITY                                    │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • Django DEBUG=False in production                           │ │
    │  │ • SECRET_KEY in .env (chmod 600, NOT in git)                │ │
    │  │ • HTTPS only (HSTS header)                                  │ │
    │  │ • Input validation, CSRF protection                          │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           ▲                                       │
    │  Layer 6: SERVICE HARDENING                                       │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • SSH: key-only auth, no root login, non-standard port      │ │
    │  │ • Nginx: hide version, limit request size                   │ │
    │  │ • PostgreSQL: localhost only, strong passwords              │ │
    │  │ • Remove unused services (telnet, ftp, rsh)                │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           ▲                                       │
    │  Layer 5: MANDATORY ACCESS CONTROL (SELinux / AppArmor)           │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • Even IF nginx is hacked, SELinux limits what it can do    │ │
    │  │ • AppArmor profiles: confine each service to its job       │ │
    │  │ • Check: aa-status, getenforce, audit2allow                │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           ▲                                       │
    │  Layer 4: INTRUSION DETECTION (fail2ban, AIDE, auditd)           │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • fail2ban: 5 failed SSH → IP banned for 1 hour             │ │
    │  │ • AIDE: detect if hackers modified system files             │ │
    │  │ • auditd: log all access to /etc/shadow, /etc/passwd       │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           ▲                                       │
    │  Layer 3: FIREWALL (iptables/nftables/ufw/firewalld)             │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • Default: DENY all incoming                                 │ │
    │  │ • Allow: 22 (SSH), 80 (HTTP), 443 (HTTPS) ONLY              │ │
    │  │ • PostgreSQL (5432): NEVER exposed to internet               │ │
    │  │ • ufw status verbose → verify rules weekly                  │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           ▲                                       │
    │  Layer 2: USER & PERMISSION SECURITY                              │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • Least privilege: anihpj user has NO shell, NO sudo       │ │
    │  │ • SSH: key-only, no passwords, no root login                │ │
    │  │ • sudo: specific commands only, never NOPASSWD: ALL        │ │
    │  │ • umask 027 for service users                               │ │
    │  │ • Audit: find / -perm -4000 (SUID files), check cron jobs  │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           ▲                                       │
    │  Layer 1: PHYSICAL SECURITY & UPDATES                             │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ • unattended-upgrades: auto-install security patches        │ │
    │  │ • apt update && apt upgrade weekly (or automatic)           │ │
    │  │ • Disk encryption (LUKS) if physical access is a risk       │ │
    │  │ • BIOS password, secure boot enabled                        │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                                                                   │
    │  🎯 KEY INSIGHT: If ONE layer fails, the NEXT layer still        │
    │     protects you. Defense in Depth = no single point of failure.  │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 31: SELinux & AppArmor — Mandatory Access Control  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    31.1  Discretionary vs Mandatory Access Control
          - DAC (regular Linux permissions): owner decides who accesses
          - MAC (SELinux/AppArmor): the system enforces policy beyond permissions
          - Even root can be restricted by MAC policies
          [ANALOGY: DAC = building keys | MAC = security guard that checks ID even with key]

    31.2  SELinux — Security-Enhanced Linux (RHEL/Rocky)  🎯
          - getenforce            → Enforcing / Permissive / Disabled
          - setenforce 0          → temporary Permissive (for debugging)
          - /etc/selinux/config   → persistent SELinux mode
          - ls -Z file            → show SELinux context (user:role:type:level)
          - ps -Z                 → process contexts
          - restorecon -Rv /var/www/anihpj/ → restore default file contexts
          - semanage fcontext     → manage file context policies
          - audit2allow           → create policy from denial log
          - ausearch -c nginx     → search audit log for nginx denials
          [TROUBLESHOOTING BOX: "SELinux is blocking nginx from reading files"]

    31.3  AppArmor — Application Armor (Ubuntu)  🎯
          - aa-status              → current AppArmor status
          - /etc/apparmor.d/       → profile directory
          - aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginx  → enforce profile
          - aa-disable /etc/apparmor.d/...             → disable profile
          - dmesg | grep apparmor  → AppArmor denials in kernel log
          [SCENARIO: AppArmor blocks Gunicorn from writing to /var/log/anihpj/]

          [SELinux vs AppArmor — COMPARISON FOR LFCS (BOTH ARE TESTED!)]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                  │
          │  FEATURE            │ SELinux                 │ AppArmor         │
          │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │  Used by            │ RHEL, Rocky, Fedora     │ Ubuntu, Debian   │
          │  LFCS distro        │ Rocky Linux 9 VM        │ Ubuntu 22.04 VM  │
          │  Approach           │ Label-based (contexts)  │ Path-based       │
          │                     │ Every FILE & PROCESS     │ (profiles)       │
          │                     │ has a security context   │ Profiles attached│
          │                     │ (user:role:type:level)   │ to executable    │
          │                     │                          │ paths            │
          │  Check status       │ getenforce              │ aa-status        │
          │  Modes              │ Enforcing/Permissive/   │ Enforce/Complain │
          │                     │ Disabled                 │ /Disabled        │
          │  Temp disable       │ setenforce 0            │ aa-teardown      │
          │  Persistent config  │ /etc/selinux/config     │ /etc/apparmor/   │
          │  View labels/profile│ ls -Z / ps -Z           │ aa-status        │
          │  Troubleshooting    │ ausearch -m avc         │ dmesg | grep     │
          │                     │ sealert -l              │ apparmor         │
          │  Restore defaults   │ restorecon -Rv /path/   │ aa-enforce /path │
          │                     │                          │ to/profile       │
          │                                                                  │
          │  🎯 LFCS EXAM TIP: You get BOTH distros on the exam!            │
          │     • Ubuntu VM → use AppArmor commands (aa-status, aa-disable) │
          │     • Rocky VM   → use SELinux commands (getenforce, ls -Z)     │
          │     • If a task says \"service can't access files\" →            │
          │       Ubuntu: check AppArmor first (dmesg | grep apparmor)      │
          │       Rocky:   check SELinux first (ausearch -m avc)            │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


Chapter 32: System Hardening — Defense in Depth  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    32.1  The Principle of Least Privilege
          - Every user, service, and process should have MINIMUM needed permissions
          - Service accounts: no home, no shell, no login
          - File permissions: tightest possible that still works
          - Network: only open ports that are actually needed
          [HARDENING CHECKLIST for anihpj server]

    32.2  Auditing with auditd  🎯
          - apt install auditd
          - /etc/audit/auditd.conf    → daemon configuration
          - /etc/audit/rules.d/       → audit rules
          - auditctl -w /etc/passwd -p rwxa → watch for changes to /etc/passwd
          - ausearch -f /etc/shadow   → search audit log for shadow file access
          - aureport                  → summary reports

    32.3  fail2ban — Automatic IP Banning  🎯
          - apt install fail2ban
          - /etc/fail2ban/jail.conf        → default rules
          - /etc/fail2ban/jail.local       → your overrides
          - [sshd] jail: maxretry=5, bantime=1h, findtime=10m
          - fail2ban-client status sshd    → view banned IPs
          - fail2ban-client set sshd unbanip 1.2.3.4 → unban IP
          [CONFIG: fail2ban protecting anihpj SSH + nginx]

    32.4  File Integrity Monitoring with AIDE
          - AIDE (Advanced Intrusion Detection Environment)
          - Initialize database: aide --init
          - Compare current state: aide --check
          - /etc/aide/aide.conf → what to monitor and how
          [USE CASE: Detect unauthorized changes to anihpj source code]

    32.5  Security Best Practices Checklist
          - ☐ SSH key-only auth (PasswordAuthentication no)
          - ☐ Dedicated service user for anihpj (no login shell)
          - ☐ Firewall: only 22, 80, 443 open
          - ☐ PostgreSQL: only localhost connections
          - ☐ Secrets in .env (chmod 600, not in Git)
          - ☐ Automatic security updates enabled
          - ☐ fail2ban active on SSH and nginx
          - ☐ Regular backup with offsite storage
          - ☐ Audit logging for sensitive files
          [COMPLETE HARDENING CHECKLIST — 30 items]


Chapter 33: Certificates, Keys & Encryption  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    33.1  Symmetric vs Asymmetric Encryption
          - Symmetric: same key for encrypt+decrypt (AES) — fast, good for bulk data
          - Asymmetric: public key encrypts, private key decrypts (RSA/Ed25519) — slower
          - Hybrid (TLS/HTTPS): asymmetric for key exchange, symmetric for data
          [DIAGRAM: How HTTPS uses both to connect to anihpj.com]

    33.2  OpenSSL — Working with Certificates  🎯
          - Generate private key: openssl genrsa -out anihpj.key 4096
          - Generate self-signed cert: openssl req -x509 -new -key anihpj.key
          - View cert info: openssl x509 -in cert.pem -text -noout
          - Check cert expiry: openssl x509 -enddate -noout -in cert.pem
          - Test TLS connection: openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443
          [SCENARIO: Create self-signed cert for internal anihpj dev server]

    33.3  GPG — Encrypting Files and Emails
          - gpg --gen-key             → generate GPG key pair
          - gpg -e -r email file      → encrypt file for recipient
          - gpg -d file.gpg           → decrypt file
          - gpg --sign file           → sign file (verify integrity)
          [USE CASE: Encrypt anihpj database backup before sending offsite]

    33.4  SSH Keys — Deep Dive  🎯
          - Key types: RSA (2048/4096), Ed25519 (recommended), ECDSA
          - ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -b 521 -C "anihpj deploy key"
          - Key permissions: 600 for private key, 644 for public key
          - SSH agent: ssh-agent + ssh-add (avoid typing passphrase repeatedly)
          - Certificate-based SSH: for large teams (ssh-keygen -s CA -I identity)

PART 10: SYSTEM MONITORING & PERFORMANCE — KEEPING TABS ON IT ALL        🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Chapter 34: System Monitoring — CPU, Memory, Disk & Network  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    34.1  CPU Monitoring
          - top / htop          → real-time CPU per process
          - mpstat -P ALL 1     → per-CPU statistics every second
          - sar -u 1 5          → CPU utilization, 5 samples, 1s interval
          - uptime              → load averages (1, 5, 15 min)
          - Load average > number of CPUs = overloaded
          [DIAGRAM: CPU load average explained — what 1.5 means on a 4-core server]

          [LOAD AVERAGE EXPLAINED — THE MOST MISUNDERSTOOD METRIC]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                  │
          │  $ uptime                                                        │
          │  14:23:01 up 45 days, 2 users, load average: 1.50, 0.80, 0.60  │
          │                                    │        │       │           │
          │                                    │        │       └─ 15 min   │
          │                                    │        └────────── 5 min   │
          │                                    └─────────────────── 1 min   │
          │                                                                  │
          │  WHAT DO THESE NUMBERS MEAN?                                     │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
          │  │  They represent the AVERAGE number of processes that     │    │
          │  │  were: 1) Running on CPU  OR  2) Waiting for CPU        │    │
          │  │  (in the \"runnable\" state — R in ps output)            │    │
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
          │                                                                  │
          │  On a 4-CORE server:                                             │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
          │  │  Load 0.00 — 100% idle                                  │    │
          │  │  Load 1.00 — 1 core fully utilized (25% of capacity)    │    │
          │  │  Load 2.00 — 2 cores fully utilized (50% of capacity)   │    │
          │  │  Load 4.00 — ALL 4 cores at 100% (saturated!)          │    │
          │  │  Load 8.00 — 2x oversubscribed! Processes WAITING! 😰   │    │
          │  │                                                          │    │
          │  │  RULE OF THUMB: Load < #CPUs = OK                        │    │
          │  │                 Load = #CPUs = fully utilized            │    │
          │  │                 Load > #CPUs = investigate NOW!          │    │
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ANIHPJ EXAMPLE: 4-core server, load = 4.50                     │
          │  → top shows: 3 gunicorn workers @ 100% + postgres @ 80%       │
          │  → Action: Increase gunicorn workers or add CPU cores            │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    34.2  Memory Monitoring
          - free -h             → RAM and swap usage
          - vmstat 1            → virtual memory statistics every second
          - sar -r 1 5          → memory report
          - /proc/meminfo       → detailed kernel memory breakdown
          - smem                → per-process memory (accurate with shared libs)
          [SCENARIO: Gunicorn workers using too much RAM — identify the issue]

    34.3  Disk I/O Monitoring
          - iostat -x 1         → disk I/O statistics every second
          - iotop               → top-like view for disk I/O
          - sar -d 1 5          → disk activity report
          - hdparm -t /dev/sda  → raw disk read speed
          [SCENARIO: PostgreSQL slow queries — investigate disk I/O bottleneck]

    34.4  Network Monitoring
          - iftop                → bandwidth per connection (like top for network)
          - nethogs              → bandwidth per process
          - sar -n DEV 1        → network interface statistics
          - ss -i               → socket statistics with internals
          - tcpdump -i eth0 -c 100 → capture 100 packets for analysis

    34.5  System-Wide Status Commands  🎯
          - dmesg | tail -20     → last 20 kernel messages (errors, hardware events)
          - lscpu                → CPU info
          - lsmem                → memory layout
          - lspci                → PCI devices
          - lsusb                → USB devices
          - dmidecode            → BIOS and hardware info


Chapter 35: Performance Tuning & Kernel Parameters  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    35.1  sysctl — Kernel Runtime Parameters  🎯
          - sysctl -a                        → list all kernel parameters
          - sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward       → view one parameter
          - sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1  → change temporarily
          - /etc/sysctl.conf                 → persistent changes
          - /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf    → drop-in file
          - sysctl -p                        → apply sysctl.conf now
          [TABLE: Important kernel parameters for web servers]

    35.2  ulimits — Resource Limits for Processes  🎯
          - ulimit -a            → show current limits
          - ulimit -n 65536      → increase open file limit (for nginx/postgres)
          - /etc/security/limits.conf → persistent per-user/group limits
          - /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service → LimitNOFILE= for systemd services

    35.3  Tuning for anihpj in Production
          - sysctl net.core.somaxconn = 1024  → more queued connections
          - vm.swappiness = 10                 → prefer RAM over swap
          - fs.file-max = 200000               → system-wide file descriptor limit
          - nginx worker_processes auto        → match CPU count
          - nginx worker_connections 1024      → per-worker connection limit
          [COMPLETE TUNING PROFILE for anihpj Django web server]


Chapter 36: Troubleshooting — Finding and Fixing Problems  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    36.1  The Troubleshooting Methodology
          - Define: what is the exact symptom?
          - Isolate: which layer is the problem in?
          - Test: change one thing at a time, verify each step
          - Fix: apply the minimal change needed
          - Verify: confirm the fix works and nothing else broke
          [FLOWCHART: Generic Linux troubleshooting decision tree]

    36.2  Boot Problems  🎯
          - Can't boot? → GRUB rescue → emergency.target → rescue.target
          - Read-only filesystem → fsck
          - Wrong default boot entry → /etc/default/grub
          - Kernel panic → boot previous kernel from GRUB menu
          [RECOVERY SCENARIOS: 5 common boot failure types + solutions]

          [BOOT FAILURE RECOVERY — DECISION FLOW]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                  │
          │  SERVER WON'T BOOT — What do you see?                            │
          │                                                                  │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  BLACK SCREEN (no GRUB menu)                                 ││
          │  │  → Boot from Ubuntu Live ISO/USB                             ││
          │  │  → mount /dev/sda1 /mnt                                      ││
          │  │  → grub-install --boot-directory=/mnt/boot /dev/sda          ││
          │  │  → update-grub (from chroot)                                 ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                  │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  GRUB RESCUE PROMPT (grub>)                                  ││
          │  │  → ls (find partitions)                                      ││
          │  │  → set root=(hd0,msdos1)  (or hd0,gpt2 for GPT)             ││
          │  │  → linux /vmlinuz root=/dev/sda1                              ││
          │  │  → initrd /initrd.img                                        ││
          │  │  → boot                                                      ││
          │  │  After boot: update-grub && grub-install /dev/sda            ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                  │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  KERNEL PANIC (scrolling error messages)                     ││
          │  │  → Reboot, hold SHIFT at GRUB                                ││
          │  │  → Select "Advanced options" → previous kernel version      ││
          │  │  → Boot old kernel → apt update && apt upgrade               ││
          │  │  → Check: uname -r (new kernel may be buggy)                ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                  │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  EMERGENCY MODE (root password prompt)                       ││
          │  │  → Enter root password                                       ││
          │  │  → journalctl -xb (see boot errors)                          ││
          │  │  → mount -o remount,rw /  (make root writable)              ││
          │  │  → Fix the issue (broken fstab entry? bad systemd unit?)    ││
          │  │  → systemctl reboot                                          ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                  │
          │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │  FILESYSTEM ERRORS (fsck required)                           ││
          │  │  → Boot to recovery/emergency mode                           ││
          │  │  → umount /dev/sda1  (must be unmounted!)                   ││
          │  │  → fsck -y /dev/sda1 (auto-repair)                          ││
          │  │  → fsck -f /dev/sda1 (force check even if "clean")         ││
          │  │  → reboot                                                    ││
          │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                                  │
          │  🎯 LFCS EXAM: You may be given a BROKEN VM that won't boot.    │
          │     Your task: diagnose and fix it using these techniques.       │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    36.3  Service Won't Start  🎯
          - systemctl status service    → see error message
          - journalctl -u service -n 50 → detailed logs
          - service --dry-run           → what would it do
          - config test: nginx -t, sshd -t, named-checkconf
          [FLOWCHART: "nginx won't start" → step-by-step diagnosis]

    36.4  Disk Full / Out of Space  🎯
          - df -hT                      → which filesystem is full?
          - du -sh /*                   → find largest directories
          - du -ah /var | sort -rh | head → find largest files
          - find / -name "*.log" -size +100M → large log files
          - journalctl --vacuum-size=500M → shrink journal
          - clear package cache: apt clean
          [SCENARIO: anihpj server runs out of disk space at 3am — fix it]

    36.5  Network Troubleshooting  🎯
          - Can't reach server? → ping → traceroute → check firewall → check service
          - Port not responding? → ss -tulnp → firewall rules → service status
          - DNS not resolving? → /etc/resolv.conf → systemd-resolved → dig test
          [TROUBLESHOOTING LADDER: Layer 1 (physical) → Layer 7 (application)]

    36.6  Permission Denied Errors  🎯
          - ls -la to check permissions
          - stat file → full metadata
          - id user → what groups they're in
          - SELinux/AppArmor: check audit log
          [DECISION TREE: "Permission denied" → find root cause]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 11: PRODUCTION & AUTOMATION — RUNNING THE FULL SHOW                 🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    ⚡ AUTOMATION CHOICES — CRON vs SYSTEMD TIMER vs AT
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                   │
    │  "I need to run a task on a schedule. Which tool?"               │
    │                                                                   │
    │  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │  FEATURE     │ CRON            │ SYSTEMD TIMER              │ │
    │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │ │
    │  │  Config      │ crontab -e       │ .timer + .service files   │ │
    │  │  Precision   │ 1 minute         │ 1 second (OnUnitActiveSec)│ │
    │  │  Logging     │ /var/log/syslog  │ journalctl -u timer-name  │ │
    │  │  Dependencies│ None (DIY)       │ Requires=, After=         │ │
    │  │  Random delay│ Rare             │ RandomizedDelaySec=       │ │
    │  │  Calendar    │ ✅ 5-field syntax │ ✅ OnCalendar=             │ │
    │  │  Missed jobs │ cron ignores     │ Persistent=true catches up│ │
    │  │  Monitoring  │ Manual           │ systemctl list-timers     │ │
    │  │  Best for    │ Simple schedules │ Complex, service-integrated│ │
    │  └──────────────┴─────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                                                                   │
    │  USE AT for ONE-TIME tasks:                                        │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ at 02:00                    → "run this at 2 AM tonight"    │ │
    │  │ at now + 30 minutes         → "run this in half an hour"    │ │
    │  │ at 02:00 tomorrow           → "run this tomorrow at 2 AM"   │ │
    │  │ atq                         → list pending at jobs           │ │
    │  │ atrm 3                      → remove job #3                 │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                                                                   │
    │  ANIHPJ AUTOMATION PLAN:                                          │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │  TASK                  │ TOOL         │ SCHEDULE             │ │
    │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │ │
    │  │  Database backup       │ cron         │ Daily @ 2:00 AM      │ │
    │  │  Log rotation          │ logrotate    │ Daily (via cron.d)   │ │
    │  │  Health check          │ systemd timer│ Every 5 minutes      │ │
    │  │  SSL cert renewal      │ certbot+cron │ Twice daily          │ │
    │  │  apt security updates  │ unattended   │ Daily                │ │
    │  │  Maintenance restart   │ at           │ Next Sunday 3 AM     │ │
    │  │  Clean old backups     │ cron         │ Weekly @ Sunday 4 AM │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


    🚀 ANIHPJ DEPLOYMENT PIPELINE — FROM CODE TO PRODUCTION
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                                   │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │  PHASE 1: PREPARE (local/dev machine)                       │ │
    │  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐ │ │
    │  │  │ git push │───▶│ CI checks │───▶│ docker build & push  │ │ │
    │  │  │ to main  │    │ (tests)   │    │ to registry          │ │ │
    │  │  └──────────┘    └───────────┘    └──────────────────────┘ │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           │                                       │
    │                           ▼                                       │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │  PHASE 2: DEPLOY (production server via SSH)                │ │
    │  │                                                               │ │
    │  │  1️⃣  ssh deploy@anihpj-prod                                   │ │
    │  │  2️⃣  cd /var/www/anihpj && git pull origin main              │ │
    │  │  3️⃣  source venv/bin/activate                                 │ │
    │  │  4️⃣  pip install -r requirements.txt                          │ │
    │  │  5️⃣  python manage.py migrate          (Ch 28)               │ │
    │  │  6️⃣  python manage.py collectstatic --noinput                │ │
    │  │  7️⃣  sudo systemctl restart anihpj      (Ch 16)              │ │
    │  │  8️⃣  systemctl status anihpj — verify!                       │ │
    │  │  9️⃣  curl -I https://anihpj.com/jobs/ → 200 OK              │ │
    │  │  🔟  journalctl -u anihpj -f (watch for errors)              │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                           │                                       │
    │                           ▼                                       │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │  PHASE 3: VERIFY & MONITOR                                   │ │
    │  │  ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ │ │
    │  │  │Health check│ │Log watch │ │Rollback  │ │Notify team  │ │ │
    │  │  │200 OK ✅   │ │No errors │ │plan ready│ │Slack/email  │ │ │
    │  │  └────────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └─────────────┘ │ │
    │  │                                                               │ │
    │  │  IF DEPLOYMENT FAILS:                                         │ │
    │  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │ │
    │  │  │ 1. git checkout <previous-commit>                     │   │ │
    │  │  │ 2. sudo systemctl restart anihpj                      │   │ │
    │  │  │ 3. Verify: curl -I https://anihpj.com → 200 OK       │   │ │
    │  │  │ 4. Investigate: journalctl -u anihpj -n 100           │   │ │
    │  │  │ 5. Fix, test in dev, redeploy                        │   │ │
    │  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │ │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                                                                   │
    │  🎯 LFCS RELEVANCE: Every command in this pipeline is tested!     │
    │     ssh, git, pip, python, systemctl, curl, journalctl — all LFCS │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 37: Cron & Scheduled Tasks — Automating Recurring Work  🎯
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    37.1  cron — The Standard Task Scheduler  🎯
          - crontab -e         → edit your crontab
          - crontab -l         → list current crontab
          - crontab -r         → remove all cron jobs ⚠️ IRREVERSIBLE
          - /etc/crontab       → system-wide crontab (with user column)
          - /etc/cron.d/       → drop-in crontab files
          - /etc/cron.daily/   → daily scripts (run automatically)
          - /etc/cron.weekly/  → weekly scripts
          - /etc/cron.monthly/ → monthly scripts

          [CRON TIME FORMAT — the 5 fields]
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │                                                                  │
          │  * * * * *  /path/to/command                                    │
          │  │ │ │ │ │                                                       │
          │  │ │ │ │ └── Day of week (0=Sun, 1=Mon ... 6=Sat)               │
          │  │ │ │ └──── Month (1-12)                                        │
          │  │ │ └────── Day of month (1-31)                                 │
          │  │ └──────── Hour (0-23)                                         │
          │  └────────── Minute (0-59)                                       │
          │                                                                  │
          │  EXAMPLES for anihpj:                                            │
          │  0 2 * * *   /scripts/backup-anihpj.sh    → 2 AM daily backup  │
          │  */5 * * * * /scripts/health-check.sh     → every 5 minutes    │
          │  0 0 * * 0   /scripts/rotate-logs.sh      → Sundays midnight   │
          │  30 6 1 * *  /scripts/monthly-report.sh   → 1st of month 6:30  │
          │                                                                  │
          │  💡 Use: crontab.guru to visualize cron expressions             │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    37.2  anacron — Run Missed Jobs  🎯
          - cron requires server to be on at exact time
          - anacron runs jobs even if server was off (on next boot)
          - /etc/anacrontab → config: PERIOD  DELAY  JOB-ID  COMMAND
          - Good for: daily/weekly jobs on servers that may be rebooted
          [COMPARISON TABLE: cron vs anacron — when to use each]

    37.3  systemd Timers — Modern Alternative to Cron  🎯
          - .timer unit file + .service unit file pair
          - OnCalendar=daily, OnBootSec=5min, OnUnitActiveSec=1h
          - systemctl list-timers → see all scheduled timers
          - Advantage over cron: integrated with journalctl logging, dependencies
          [COMPLETE TIMER UNIT for anihpj backup job]

    37.4  at — One-Time Scheduled Commands
          - at 14:30 → run command at 2:30 PM
          - at now + 1 hour → run one hour from now
          - atq → list pending at jobs
          - atrm job_id → remove pending job
          [USE CASE: Schedule anihpj maintenance window restart]


Chapter 38: Full anihpj Linux Server — Production Deployment
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    38.1  Complete Server Build — Step by Step
          - Phase 1: Base system setup
            ○ Install Ubuntu 22.04 LTS
            ○ Update packages, set hostname, timezone
            ○ Create admin user, configure SSH keys
            ○ Disable root SSH login, enable UFW
          - Phase 2: Application dependencies
            ○ Install Python 3.11, pip, venv
            ○ Install PostgreSQL 15
            ○ Install Nginx, Certbot
            ○ Install Redis (for Celery)
          - Phase 3: Application setup
            ○ Create anihpj service user
            ○ Clone/deploy source code
            ○ Set up virtual environment
            ○ Configure .env (chmod 600)
            ○ Create PostgreSQL database and user
            ○ Run migrations, collectstatic
          - Phase 4: Services
            ○ Create and enable anihpj.service (Gunicorn)
            ○ Configure Nginx virtual host
            ○ Obtain SSL certificate
            ○ Configure firewall rules
          - Phase 5: Monitoring & Maintenance
            ○ Set up log rotation
            ○ Create backup cron jobs
            ○ Configure fail2ban
            ○ Set up health check script
          [COMPLETE BASH AUTOMATION SCRIPT for full server setup]

    38.2  Post-Deployment Verification Checklist
          - ☐ curl http://anihpj.com/jobs/ returns 200
          - ☐ HTTPS redirect works
          - ☐ SSL certificate valid and A-rated
          - ☐ systemctl status anihpj → active (running)
          - ☐ systemctl status nginx → active (running)
          - ☐ systemctl status postgresql → active (running)
          - ☐ UFW: only 22, 80, 443 open (ufw status verbose)
          - ☐ PostgreSQL: only localhost connections
          - ☐ Backup runs (test: /scripts/backup-anihpj.sh)
          - ☐ Log rotation configured (logrotate --debug)
          - ☐ No 'D' state processes (ps aux | grep ' D ')
          - ☐ Disk usage < 70% (df -hT)
          [HEALTH REPORT SCRIPT: outputs a coloured pass/fail for each check]

    38.3  Common Production Issues and Fixes
          [TABLE of 20 most common problems with diagnosis steps and fixes]
          | PROBLEM                          | DIAGNOSIS                  | FIX                           |
          | 502 Bad Gateway from Nginx       | journalctl -u anihpj       | Restart gunicorn service      |
          | PostgreSQL connection refused    | systemctl status postgresql | Check pg_hba.conf             |
          | Permission denied: media files   | ls -la /var/www/anihpj/    | chown anihpj:www-data         |
          | Site slow, high load average     | top, iostat                | Check workers, DB queries     |
          | Disk full                        | df -h, du -sh /*           | Clear logs, rotate, expand LV |
          | SSH: too many auth failures      | fail2ban-client status     | Unban IP or whitelist yours   |
          | Django settings error at boot   | journalctl -u anihpj -n 50 | Check .env file, perms        |
          | Static files 404               | nginx error.log            | collectstatic, check alias    |
          | SSL cert expired               | openssl x509 -enddate      | certbot renew                 |
          | OOM killer killed gunicorn     | dmesg | grep oom           | Reduce workers, add RAM/swap  |
